#### Security (4)

- **[MED]** Group message path calls Anthropic with zero rate limiting — `src/app/api/linq/webhook/route.ts:543-551`
  Security Rule: "All routes that call the Anthropic API must have rate limiting." The 1:1 path enforces this at message-router.ts:217 (isUserRateLimited, 20 msg/5min per user) BEFORE processTextMessage. The group path has no equivalent. handleInboundMessage -> evaluateGate -> processGroupMessage (group-bridge.ts:231 anthropicFetch) runs with NO per-user or per-chat rate ceiling. I confirmed this: grep for isUserRateLimited / rateLimit / limiter across group-bridge.ts, group-detection.ts, group-state.ts, group-gate.ts, route.ts, and persona-pipeline.ts returns zero matches in the group path. The group gate (group-gate.ts:102-154) is a CONTENT filter, not an abuse filter, and it does not close the hole: evaluateGate step 1 (mentionsPersona, line 116) returns SEND_TO_PERSONA and BYPASSES the 30s cooldown check at step 3. So a participant in an active group who repeats "Jove ..." on every message forces one billed Sonnet call per inbound message with no upper bound. Webhook signature verification (route.ts:634) gates who can POST, but Linq relays arbitrary group-member text, so a hostile or malfunctioning group member is inside the trust boundary. Downstream consequence: unbounded Anthropic spend and a Vercel-function-time amplification vector via a documented-but-deprecated endpoint that is still live.
  _→ Add a per-owner-user (or per-chat) in-memory rate check in handleInboundMessage before processGroupMessage, mirroring isUserRateLimited in message-router.ts. Key it on groupState.owner_user_id so it shares the abuse budget with the 1:1 path, or on chatId if you want a separate group budget. Add a test in group-gate.test.ts's sibling proving the Nth rapid direct-address message in a window is dropped before anthropicFetch. CONFIDENCE: high that the limiter is absent; the abuse-amplification severity assumes the endpoint stays live, which the file header confirms ("Do not delete until group facilitator is ported or retired")._
  <sub>verified (high): Mechanically real and verified end-to-end. `isUserRateLimited` is defined and called ONLY on the 1:1 fork (src/lib/linq/message-router.ts:82,217); the Postgres daily cap (checkDailyMessageLimit) is called ONLY on the web path (src/app/api/chat/route.ts:121). The group fork (webhook route.ts:431 `if (isGroup)` → processGroupMessage at route.ts:545 → anthropicFetch at group-bridge.ts:231) hits neither, and processGroupMessage has no internal throttle. evaluateGate step 1 (group-gate.ts:116, mentionsPersona) returns SEND_TO_PERSONA before the 30s cooldown check at line 126, so repeating "Jove"/"sage" forces one billed Sonnet call per inbound message with no ceiling — a literal violation of the Security Rule "All routes that call the Anthropic API must have rate limiting." The path is genuinely live (ADR-035/041, state.md:238 — group facilitator permanently on Linq), and Linq relays arbitrary group-member text inside the signature-gated boundary. I downgrade high→medium (Should-fix, not a blocker) because: (1) exploitation requires being an accepted member of an ACTIVE facilitated iMessage group — socially gated, not an open endpoint; (2) this repo already treats unbounded Anthropic exposure on secondary/deprecated paths as tracked beta debt, not DO-NOT-SHIP — Upstash is failing open in prod right now and the single documented 🚨 beta-blocker is scoped to OTP only, with the group facilitator explicitly "untouched/future work"; (3) worst case is one bad actor in one group, bounded by the group timeout and [NO_RESPONSE] short-circuit — the "fleet-wide amplification" framing overstates it. Fix is small (key the limiter by senderPhone/chatId before processGroupMessage, or move the cooldown ahead of the mentionsPersona branch). The mechanical verification is high-confidence; the exact severity depends on real group-facilitator usage volume, which the unfilled Beta Users section hides — if groups are used at any scale with non-trusted participants, this rises toward the top of Should-fix.</sub>
- **[MED]** Raw phone numbers / handles logged in group webhook and detection — `src/app/api/linq/webhook/route.ts:194`
  Security Rule: "Never log user message content, phone numbers, or auth tokens. Log event types, IDs, and counts only." Three call sites log phone numbers: (1) route.ts:191-196 logs addedHandle (a raw participant phone/handle from participant.added); (2) route.ts:224-229 logs removedHandle (raw phone from participant.removed); (3) group-detection.ts:103-108 logs nonPersonaHandles, an array of normalized E.164 phone numbers, via handles=%j. I verified the exact args: line 194 passes addedHandle, line 107 passes the nonPersonaHandles array. These are PII writes to the log stream on routine group-lifecycle events, not error-only paths. The 1:1 router was already hardened against this (it logs user_id, not phone), so this is group-path drift from an established discipline.
  _→ Drop the handle/handles values from these three log lines; log counts and chat_id only (e.g. participants=%d is already fine at group-detection.ts:104, just remove the handles=%j argument). If a hashed/last-4 identifier is needed for debugging participant churn, derive one rather than logging the raw E.164 string. CONFIDENCE: high — these are unambiguous phone-number values reaching console.log._
  <sub>verified (high): Confirmed in the current tree. Three live, reachable call sites log raw phone numbers/handles, violating CLAUDE.md:100 ("Never log user message content, phone numbers, or auth tokens"): route.ts:191-196 logs addedHandle and route.ts:224-229 logs removedHandle (both dispatched at route.ts:154-159), and src/lib/linq/group-detection.ts:103-108 logs the normalized-E.164 nonPersonaHandles array via handles=%j. These are routine group-lifecycle logs, not error-only paths, and the Linq webhook stays permanently live for group facilitator per ADR-035 (decisions.md:216/262), so "deprecated for 1:1" does not put it out of scope. Two bookkeeping corrections that do not change the verdict: the finding cites src/lib/persona/group-detection.ts but the file is src/lib/linq/group-detection.ts (line numbers correct), and the trace field is event.trace_id not event.event_id. Medium is correct: logs are access-controlled (no RLS) and it is a direct documented Security Rule violation and group-path drift from the ADR-037 redaction discipline, but not a Blocker-grade external data-loss/auth hole.</sub>
- **[LOW]** Full Linq API response body dumped via data=%j (echoes outbound message text) — `src/lib/linq/sender.ts:114-120`
  sendMessage logs the full Linq response with data=%j on EVERY send (success and failure), and createChat does the same at sender.ts:232-237. The POST /chats/{chatId}/messages response echoes the message parts that were just sent, so this dumps outbound text into logs. For the group path that text is Jove's reply; for createChat it is the initial outbound. This is outbound assistant content, not inbound user-message content, so it is arguably outside the letter of "Never log user message content" — but it is an unsanitized full-payload dump (%j) that can carry user-derived fields (e.g. a sender label echoed back, recipient handles in some Linq response shapes) and is inconsistent with the IDs/counts-only logging discipline followed elsewhere. Note for contrast: linqFetch itself is disciplined — success logs status only (sender.ts:54-60), and the error branch logs err not the full body (sender.ts:46-52). The leak is specifically the two explicit data=%j lines in sendMessage and createChat.
  _→ Replace data=%j in sender.ts:115 and sender.ts:233 with the already-extracted messageId/chatId plus status (the function already parses messageId at line 123 and chatId at 241-247). This was a debugging aid ("Log full response for debugging" / "Log the full response so we can see Linq's actual format") that should not survive into steady-state. CONFIDENCE: high on the dump; medium on whether it ever contains true user PII, since it depends on Linq's exact response schema for these two endpoints._
  <sub>verified (high): Confirmed the code exactly as cited: src/lib/linq/sender.ts:114-120 logs the full Linq response with data=%j on every sendMessage call (success and failure, after the retry block), and createChat repeats it at :232-237, while linqFetch itself is disciplined (status-only success at :54-60, err-only error at :46-52). It is a real, currently-present issue, but as a consistency/discipline defect, not as the headline "echoes outbound message text" leak: the payload is the response body (the consumer only reads id/message_id off it at :122-123), and whether Linq echoes the sent text or a phone handle back is unverified inference about a third-party response shape — the finding itself concedes this is outbound Jove/system content, not inbound user-message content, so it does not violate the "never log user message content" rule. What makes it legitimately should-track-and-clean is the project's own ADR-037 (decisions.md:226-231), which already ruled outbound provider-response bodies must be suppressed/redacted (it omits raw_payload on outbound rows precisely because the provider echoes the full body) — these two leftover "for debugging" %j lines are inconsistent with that settled discipline and with the IDs/counts-only logging followed everywhere else. Severity is correctly low: this is console.log to access-controlled Vercel logs on the deprecating Linq group path (ADR-035; registry marks Linq deprecated), not DB-persisted PII. One thing I cannot verify and that warrants a real Linq-response capture before relying on "low": if Linq's message-send/chat-create response actually returns a recipient phone handle, the phone-number clause of the security rule is engaged and severity rises.</sub>
- **[LOW]** Reserved-keyword handler logs the raw inbound text (uppercased) — `src/lib/linq/message-router.ts:154`
  console.log("[router] reserved command received: %s (raw=%s)", command, textContent.toUpperCase().trim()) logs the user's raw inbound message text. In practice this only fires when resolveKeyword (line 62-65) returns non-null, and that requires the ENTIRE normalized message to exactly equal a keyword alias (STOP/UNSUBSCRIBE/HELP/YES/etc.), so the logged raw value is effectively always a reserved keyword phrase, not free-form user content. That makes it borderline rather than a clear violation. Flagging it because it is the one place in the inbound path that logs message-derived text at all, and the 'raw=%s' was added for alias-debugging that telemetry no longer needs.
  _→ Drop the raw=%s argument and log only the resolved command keyword (which is already a fixed enum value, not user content). CONFIDENCE: high that raw text is logged; medium that it matters, because the resolve gate constrains the value to a keyword set._
  <sub>verified (high): The line is present and unredacted: git blame shows `(raw=%s)` logging `textContent.toUpperCase().trim()` was added in dde8415 (a legal/SMS-compliance pass) to a log that previously printed only the safe `command` constant; no later commit removed it. The finding's core reasoning is correct and verified: line 154 sits inside the `if (command)` block (line 145), and `command` is non-null only when resolveKeyword (line 62-65) exact-matches the FULL normalized message against one of 14 fixed CTIA reserved-keyword aliases — so the logged raw value is always a reserved keyword (STOP/HELP/YES/UNSUBSCRIBE/etc.), never free-form user content, and carries no PII or conversational text. It is technically outside the Security Rule's "event types, IDs, and counts only" allowance (the rule's intent, per decisions.md:229/ADR-035, is preventing inner-life/PII leakage), and it is the only text-logging site in the inbound path, so it's a legitimate should-fix telemetry-hygiene item (drop raw=%s; the command constant already identifies the keyword). But it is genuinely at the severity floor — real but barely, with effectively zero PII blast radius — so I confirm it as real and keep severity low.</sub>

#### Conflicts (5)

- **[MED]** getOrCreateGroupConversation race cannot dedupe — no unique constraint on conversations.linq_group_chat_id, so duplicate group conversations are created — `/Users/jeffwaters/mywalnut/src/lib/linq/group-bridge.ts:339-365`
  getOrCreateGroupConversation does read-then-insert: SELECT by linq_group_chat_id, and if absent INSERT a new conversations row. The catch-block at :352 claims to handle the race ('another request may have created it') by re-querying. But this only works if the INSERT actually FAILS for the loser. Schema check (20260417000000_squash_baseline.sql:496-499) shows conversations.linq_group_chat_id has only a PLAIN btree index (idx_conversations_linq_group_chat_id), NOT a unique index. Compare to linq_group_chats.linq_chat_id which DOES have `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX idx_linq_group_chats_chat_id` (line 506) — that is why createGroupState's 23505 guard works. With no unique constraint, two concurrent group messages (the same high-concurrency scenario as above) both SELECT empty, both INSERT, and BOTH succeed — producing two active conversations for one group chat. The error-driven re-query at :352 never triggers because there's no error. Downstream: messages split across two conversation rows; processGroupMessage loads history from whichever conversation its prefetch landed on (group-bridge.ts:180-184), so Jove sees a fragmented transcript missing the messages that went to the sibling conversation. ADR-027 explicitly accepts duplicate active conversations for the 1:1 path because 'Supabase allows multiple active conversations per user by design' — but that reasoning is about the user's session history, where the retry still lands both messages in ONE winning row. Here there is no winning row; the comment at :351-352 promises dedup the schema cannot deliver.
  _→ Add a unique constraint/index on conversations.linq_group_chat_id (partial, WHERE linq_group_chat_id IS NOT NULL), then the existing catch-block re-query becomes correct. Alternatively document that group conversations may fragment under concurrency. A regression test should mock two concurrent getOrCreateGroupConversation calls returning empty SELECT and assert only one conversation id results. Note: this is the same root pattern ADR-027 addresses for 1:1 but the group path lacks the constraint that ADR-027's reasoning implicitly relies on._
  <sub>verified (high): Verified real. conversations.linq_group_chat_id has only a plain partial btree index (idx_conversations_linq_group_chat_id, squash_baseline.sql:499) plus a FK — no unique index, and no later migration adds one. So the catch-block dedup at group-bridge.ts:352 (which only fires `if (error)`) is inert for the concurrent case: two webhooks both SELECT empty (group-bridge.ts:320-326), both INSERT (:339-347), both succeed, neither errors, neither re-queries — producing two active conversation rows for one group chat. The finding's comparison is accurate: createGroupState's 23505 guard (group-state.ts:67) works only because linq_group_chats.linq_chat_id IS uniquely indexed (line 506); the conversation path's mirror guard lacks that index. ADR-027's promise that 'the retry ensures both messages land in the same conversation' (decisions.md:168) genuinely does not hold here because there is no failing insert to converge on. Downstream fragmentation is real: processGroupMessage loads history by conversation_id from whichever prefetch row it got (group-bridge.ts:180-184), so Jove sees a split transcript. The path is live in production for groups (group facilitator stays on Linq per ADR-035), not dead code. I refined high→medium because the hazard is bounded: it can only fire on a group's first message (once any row exists, every later webhook short-circuits at :328), it needs a tight concurrent-miss window, and it lives on a deprecating, low-traffic provider. Real data-integrity defect with no self-healing, but narrow — should-fix, not a blocker. Fix is the one ADR-027 already implies: a partial unique index, which makes the existing :352 guard live. The exact Postgres serialization behavior of the two concurrent inserts under Edge is the one piece worth a real concurrency test before sizing the fix.</sub>
- **[LOW]** messages_since_persona_spoke counter is read-then-write, not atomic — concurrent group messages corrupt the gate counter — `/Users/jeffwaters/mywalnut/src/app/api/linq/webhook/route.ts:511-513`
  The group counter is computed in application code as `const currentCount = (groupState.messages_since_persona_spoke || 0) + 1;` then written via `updateGroupState(chatId, { messages_since_persona_spoke: currentCount })`. updateGroupState (group-state.ts:80-105) issues a plain `.update({...})` with no atomic increment, no RPC, no optimistic-version check. Group chats are exactly the high-concurrency case: multiple human participants can fire messages within the same second, and Linq delivers each as a separate webhook POST. Each handler reads the SAME stale groupState (each calls getGroupState independently at route.ts:433), computes the same currentCount, and the writes clobber each other (last-write-wins). With N simultaneous messages the counter advances by 1 instead of N. Downstream consequence: the GATE_NUDGE_MESSAGES>=6 safety net (group-gate.ts:136) that guarantees Jove never goes silent forever is defeated — the counter under-counts, so the nudge fires late or never in a busy group. The GATE_REDUCED_THRESHOLD drop after GATE_MIN_MESSAGES (group-gate.ts:141) is similarly delayed. This is the canonical concurrent-access desync: a hook/handler holding a value the DB also owns, mutated non-atomically.
  _→ Replace the read-then-write with an atomic DB increment (a Postgres RPC doing `update ... set messages_since_persona_spoke = messages_since_persona_spoke + 1 ... returning *`), and read the returned value for the gate. Add a concurrency test that fires two prefetch+update cycles against one mocked group row and asserts the counter lands at 2, not 1. Given this is a deprecated path, tracking it is acceptable, but the silence-forever failure is the user-visible consequence to note._
  <sub>verified (high): The mechanical claim is confirmed. The webhook reads groupState.messages_since_persona_spoke (route.ts:433/511), adds 1 in app code, and writes it back via updateGroupState (route.ts:513), which issues a plain .update({...}) with no atomic increment, no RPC, and no version check (group-state.ts:97-100). I verified there is no DB trigger or RPC for the counter (squash_baseline.sql:218 defines it as a plain integer column) and no per-chat serialization — each Linq POST is an independent invocation sharing only an event_id-keyed in-memory dedup cache (route.ts:57), which does not serialize distinct messages. So concurrent same-group messages do race and the counter under-counts (and the reset-to-0 write at route.ts:558-561 has the same race). Two corrections to the finding: the files are src/lib/linq/, not src/lib/persona/ (line numbers still match); and the path is NOT out of scope despite the 'deprecated' label — ADR-035 (decisions.md:212-217) keeps the Linq group facilitator permanently live. I refined severity to low (Consider), not high: the counter only feeds the GATE_NUDGE_MESSAGES backstop and the threshold drop (group-gate.ts:136/141); the primary trigger is the per-message score, computed fresh and unaffected by the race, so the user-facing behavior (cooldown + score paths) does not depend on counter exactness. Worst case is a delayed/occasionally-missed backstop nudge in a low-signal group on a path the founder is winding down — real, worth tracking, but no data loss, no security issue, no discipline breach. Confidence is high on the existence of the race; the precise real-world frequency is a concurrency question that warrants a real concurrent-load test rather than my read.</sub>
- **[LOW]** Group state is re-fetched independently per webhook with no version check — last_persona_spoke_at and is_active updates race across the lifecycle handlers — `/Users/jeffwaters/mywalnut/src/app/api/linq/webhook/route.ts:433-558`
  Every handler path reads group state fresh (getGroupState at route.ts:433, :231, group-detection.ts:50) and writes back partial updates with no optimistic concurrency control. Two interleavings produce desync: (1) A message.received handler reads groupState with is_active=true, passes the gate, and is mid-Anthropic-call (15s timeout, group-bridge.ts:29) when a participant.removed handler sets is_active=false (route.ts:242/254/284/299). The in-flight message handler then writes `messages_since_persona_spoke:0, last_persona_spoke_at:now` at route.ts:558-561, resurrecting timestamps onto a row another handler just deactivated — and sends Jove's reply (route.ts:557) into a group that has been closed. (2) The detectAndSetupGroup re-detection path flips is_active back to true and intro_sent to false at route.ts:456 BEFORE re-running detection; if detection then fails to find an owner (route.ts:463-465) it returns without re-deactivating, leaving the group is_active=true with owner_user_id=null — a state the inbound handler treats as 'truly inactive' only via the !owner_user_id branch, but isActiveGroup (group-state.ts:110) would now report it active. This is client/server (handler/DB) state desync: each handler holds a snapshot the DB mutates underneath it.
  _→ This is hard to detect by reading and is a genuine concurrency call — recommend a real human review or an integration test harness that interleaves a participant.removed against an in-flight message.received. Minimum mitigation: re-check is_active immediately before the send at route.ts:556 (re-read or conditional update guarded on is_active=true), and make the re-detection path at route.ts:456 deactivate-on-failure symmetric. I am confident the interleaving exists; whether it occurs often enough to matter on a deprecated path is a product judgment._
  <sub>verified (high): Real defect, verified by tracing the actual code (correct paths are src/lib/linq/, not the src/lib/persona/ paths the finding cites). The linq_group_chats schema (squash_baseline.sql:211-224) has no version/lock column, and updateGroupState (group-state.ts:80-105) issues a blind last-write-wins UPDATE, so the no-optimistic-concurrency premise holds. Interleaving 2 is the strongest part and is deterministic, not even concurrency-dependent: re-detection sets is_active=true at route.ts:456, then if detectAndSetupGroup resolves to multiple owners it hits group-detection.ts:170-179, where getOrCreateState(null,...) updates only owner_user_id/count and never resets is_active; the webhook's check at route.ts:459 then fails and returns at :465, leaving the row persisted is_active=true with owner_user_id=null — exactly the desynced state described, which isActiveGroup (group-state.ts:110-113) would report active. Interleaving 1 is also real (an in-flight 15s processGroupMessage call can write last_persona_spoke_at/counter and send a reply at route.ts:556-561 onto a row a sibling participant.removed handler just set is_active=false), though the finding overstates it: that write does not touch is_active, so the group is NOT resurrected to active — the surviving harm is a stray facilitator message into a just-closed group plus a stale timestamp, not full reactivation. Refining severity medium→low: this is the explicitly deprecated, low-traffic Linq group facilitator path (route.ts:1-10, ADR-035, standing deprecation memo says it should receive no further investment), the blast radius is contained to facilitator UX with no data-loss/security/Manual-integrity impact, and it breaches no documented discipline (ADR-029 chose unique-constraint+retry only for the conversation race, not group state). Confidence high on the code defects being present; the one thing I cannot verify is real-world Linq webhook concurrency/ordering, which affects how often these fire but not whether the defect exists — a production-log check or a real concurrency test would be the way to confirm trigger frequency before investing in a fix.</sub>
- **[LOW]** Webhook dedup is in-memory only — Linq retries after a cold start or across instances re-process the same event, double-sending and double-counting — `/Users/jeffwaters/mywalnut/src/app/api/linq/webhook/route.ts:57-65,654-672`
  Idempotency for the whole webhook rests on the in-memory seenEvents Map (route.ts:57). The comment at :54-56 acknowledges it 'survives within a warm serverless instance only.' The route runs on Vercel with runtime='nodejs' (route.ts:23); serverless instances are per-invocation-cold-startable and horizontally scaled. The handler processes inline and returns 200 ok:true even on internal failure (route.ts:668-672, and the FAILURE PATHS spec confirms 'returns 200 ok:true (no retry)'). But Linq's OWN retry behavior on a slow response (the Jove call can take up to 15s in groups; the inline-processing comment at :662-665 notes there is no waitUntil) is not bounded by our 200. If Linq retries a message.received event that landed on a now-cold instance, seenEvents is empty, the event re-processes: the counter increments again (route.ts:511), the message is re-inserted into messages (group-bridge.ts:166, no dedup on content/event_id), and if the gate passes, Jove is called and a SECOND reply is sent (route.ts:557). For 1:1 the same retry re-runs routeInboundMessage and can double-send a Jove reply or re-trigger a checkpoint confirm (message-router.ts:421 confirmCheckpoint). The dedup comment itself flags the upgrade-to-DB path as deferred. This is an idempotency gap on webhook retry.
  _→ Persist event_id dedup in Postgres (an inserted-events table with a unique constraint on event_id, insert-before-process, 23505 = already handled) rather than the in-memory Map. Until then, the messages-insert and counter paths are not idempotent under cross-instance retry. Whether Linq actually retries on a returned 200 needs confirmation against Linq's webhook contract — recommend verifying Linq's retry semantics with their docs/support before deciding severity, since the in-memory Map only protects same-instance rapid retries._
  <sub>verified (medium): Real but materially overstated; refine medium→low. TRUE core: the Linq webhook's only dedup is the in-memory seenEvents Map (route.ts:57, self-documented "warm instance only" at :54-56), and the durable DB-backed dedup (messaging_events partial unique index on provider+provider_message_id) is wired ONLY into the Sendblue webhook (sendblue/route.ts:109,283) — the Linq inbound path never reads/writes messaging_events, so it genuinely lacks cold-start/cross-instance idempotency. A real Linq retry of a group message.received would double-increment messages_since_persona_spoke (route.ts:511) and re-insert into messages (group-bridge.ts:166, no dedup) and could send a second reply (route.ts:557). OVERSTATED in four ways: (1) The premise that Linq retries inbound on a slow 200 is asserted, not established — the repo documents Sendblue's retry policy precisely but contains zero evidence of Linq's inbound retry semantics, and the handler returns 200 even on failure, so only a slow-response/timeout could trigger a retry; this needs a real check against Linq docs. (2) The 1:1 checkpoint-confirm double-trigger is wrong — confirmCheckpoint is idempotent/transactional via FOR UPDATE + partial unique index on (user_id, source_message_id) (confirm-checkpoint.ts:564, migration 20260417000003), so no duplicate entry. (3) The 1:1 double-reply is largely caught by findRetryStormDuplicate (call-persona.ts:432, 30s window) on the shared pipeline. (4) It is mislabeled "conflict": no documented discipline claims Linq inbound has durable dedup — ADR-035/037 and the messaging_events migration scope it to Sendblue, and route.ts:54-56 explicitly defers the DB upgrade, making this a known/accepted limitation on a deprecated, low-traffic provider, not a drift. Net: a legitimate track-and-fix on the group path only, severity low given the deprecated surface, unverified retry trigger, and partial downstream protections.</sub>
- **[LOW]** Re-detection resets intro_sent=false before re-running detection — a concurrent message can re-send the introduction (duplicate-intro guard defeated) — `/Users/jeffwaters/mywalnut/src/app/api/linq/webhook/route.ts:456 and /Users/jeffwaters/mywalnut/src/lib/linq/group-detection.ts:62-71,186-193`
  sendIntroduction (group-detection.ts:186-193) carefully sets intro_sent=true BEFORE sending, with the comment 'prevents duplicate intros from race conditions.' The re-detection path in the inbound handler undoes that guard: at route.ts:456 it writes `{ is_active: true, intro_sent: false }` then calls detectAndSetupGroup. detectAndSetupGroup's early-return logic (group-detection.ts:62) treats is_active && !intro_sent && !owner_user_id as the 're-detection path' and falls through to re-run identification, which calls sendIntroduction again. If two persona-mention messages arrive close together on a previously-inactive group, both can hit route.ts:456, both reset intro_sent=false, and both fall through to send an intro — the very duplicate the intro_sent flag exists to prevent. The window is narrow (requires two mentions before the first re-detection's intro_sent=true write commits) and the path is the recovery-from-inactive case, so impact is a duplicate greeting, not data loss.
  _→ Gate the re-detection reset so intro_sent is only cleared when the prior state genuinely had no intro, or make the intro send conditional on a compare-and-set of intro_sent within the same statement. Low severity on a deprecated path; track rather than fix. Confident the window exists; the trigger (rapid double-mention on an inactive group) is uncommon._
  <sub>verified (high): Confirmed by tracing. route.ts:456 writes {is_active:true, intro_sent:false}; detectAndSetupGroup re-reads that mutated row, hits the re-detection early-return at group-detection.ts:62 (is_active && !intro_sent && !owner_user_id all true), finds the owner, and calls sendIntroduction at :152. sendIntroduction's guard at :190 reads the stale in-memory state.intro_sent=false (getOrCreateState returns {...existing} at :142), so it sends. updateGroupState (group-state.ts:97-100) is an unconditional UPDATE with no compare-and-swap or row lock, so two concurrent persona-mention webhooks on the same previously-inactive, no-owner group can both reset intro_sent=false and both send the intro — the duplicate-intro guard described at :184/:192 is genuinely defeated. The path is live, not dead: Linq is deprecated as product direction but CLAUDE.md/ADR-035 keeps both webhook endpoints live permanently, and it is not in docs/rules.md Dead Features, so it is in scope. Severity is correctly low: the window is narrow (two mentions before the first re-detection's intro_sent commits), it is the recovery-from-inactive case, and the worst outcome is a duplicate greeting — no data loss or security impact. Real-world frequency on this low-traffic provider path is the kind of concurrency judgment that warrants a load test rather than my read, but the code defect itself is unambiguous.</sub>

#### Failure paths (3)

- **[HIGH]** Multi-user group stays is_active=true with null owner; every later message throws in unguarded Promise.all and is silently dropped — `src/app/api/linq/webhook/route.ts:512-515 (with src/lib/linq/group-detection.ts:170-179 and src/lib/linq/group-bridge.ts:331-337)`
  When a group has 2+ owner users, detectAndSetupGroup (group-detection.ts:171) creates state via getOrCreateState(null, ...) and sends INTRO_MULTI_USER but never sets is_active=false. The linq_group_chats.is_active column defaults to true (confirmed supabase/migrations/20260417000000_squash_baseline.sql:215), so the group is left ACTIVE with owner_user_id=null. On the next inbound message, the webhook passes the is_active check (route.ts:444 sees is_active=true) and reaches route.ts:512-515, which calls prefetchGroupContext inside a Promise.all with NO try/catch. prefetchGroupContext -> getOrCreateGroupConversation finds no existing conversation, hits the `!groupState.owner_user_id` branch (group-bridge.ts:331) and throws 'Multi-user group conversations not yet supported'. The Promise.all rejects; because updateGroupState (the counter increment at line 513) is the other arm, the counter write may have already landed while the message is discarded. The throw is caught only by the top-level handleEvent wrapper (route.ts:666-670), which logs and returns 200 ok:true with no retry. Net effect: every message in a multi-user group is silently dropped forever, the messages_since_persona_spoke counter drifts upward with each dropped message, and nothing is saved or surfaced. The INTRO_MULTI_USER copy implies Jove will participate ('I keep things neutral'), but it can never respond.
  _→ Either set is_active=false in the multi-owner branch (group-detection.ts:171) if multi-user facilitation is genuinely unsupported, or wrap the Promise.all at route.ts:512 in try/catch and short-circuit when owner_user_id is null before prefetch. Add a test: active group + owner_user_id=null + inbound message must not throw and must not silently increment the counter. This is a confident finding (traced end to end); whether multi-user groups should be supported at all is a product call._
  <sub>verified (high): Real and present. The multi-owner branch at group-detection.ts:170-179 calls getOrCreateState(null,...) and sets only intro_sent:true, never is_active:false (contrast the no-owner branch at line 162 which does deactivate); createGroupState omits is_active so the DB default `is_active boolean DEFAULT true NOT NULL` (squash_baseline.sql:215) leaves the group ACTIVE with null owner. On the next message, route.ts:444 (`!is_active`) is false so the re-detection guard is skipped and execution falls to the unguarded Promise.all at 512-515; prefetchGroupContext (group-bridge.ts:60-62 -> getOrCreateGroupConversation, no conversation found at 320-326) throws at group-bridge.ts:336. That throw is between the two try blocks (533 and 544), so it is caught only at the POST handler 666-670, which logs and returns {ok:true} 200 with no retry — every multi-owner message is silently dropped. The independent updateGroupState write at line 513 commits regardless of the sibling rejection, so the counter drifts up deterministically (the finding's 'may have landed' understates it). The path is live: ADR-035 in CLAUDE.md keeps the Linq group webhook permanently active and message.received still dispatches to group handling; the '[deprecated]' label is strategic, not a disabled path. No test covers the multi-owner inbound path. High confirmed: the INTRO_MULTI_USER copy promises participation Jove can never deliver, with no surface or error signal.</sub>
- **[LOW]** Group send failure resets the gate counter and persists Jove's turn as if it was delivered (state desync); 1:1 path checks send result, group path does not — `src/app/api/linq/webhook/route.ts:556-562`
  In the SEND path, processGroupMessage already inserts Jove's reply into the messages table (group-bridge.ts:256) before returning. The webhook then calls `await sendMessage(chatId, result.responseText)` at line 557 and discards the return value. sendMessage is non-throwing by contract (ADR-035 / decisions.md:217 — returns {ok:false} on failure after its one retry), so a failed delivery does NOT enter the catch at line 575. The counter reset and last_persona_spoke_at update at lines 558-561 run unconditionally. Consequences when delivery fails: (1) the group never receives Jove's message, (2) the gate now believes Jove just spoke, so it applies the 30s cooldown (group-gate.ts:126-131) and resets messages_since_persona_spoke to 0, suppressing the next several messages, and (3) the assistant turn is saved in history, so it counts as context on future turns even though no human saw it. The 1:1 router handles the symmetric case correctly — message-router.ts:296-310 inspects sendResult.status and logs message_send_failed — so this is an inconsistency, not an accepted design stance.
  _→ Check sendMessage's return value at route.ts:557. On {ok:false}, do not reset the counter / last_persona_spoke_at (so the next message can re-trigger Jove), and log a delivery-failure line as the 1:1 path does. Consider whether the assistant message should be marked or rolled back on send failure. Confident finding._
  <sub>verified (high): The core mechanism is real and present: webhook/route.ts:557 calls sendMessage and discards the result, then runs updateGroupState (messages_since_persona_spoke:0, last_persona_spoke_at:now) unconditionally at 558-561, and group-bridge.ts:256 has already persisted Jove's turn. The sendMessage actually used here is from @/lib/linq/sender (sender.ts:73), not the ADR-035 wrapper the finding cites; it is non-throwing on Linq HTTP errors (returns {ok:false} after one retry, sender.ts:104-128, linqFetch never wraps fetch in try/catch), so a 4xx/5xx delivery failure skips the catch at line 575 and the gate desyncs (cooldown SKIP at group-gate.ts:126-131). So the failure path is genuine. But the finding has errors: wrong file paths (actual files are src/lib/linq/group-bridge.ts and src/lib/linq/message-router.ts, not src/lib/persona/...), wrong contract citation (decisions.md:217/ADR-035 describes the unified send.ts wrapper, which this path does not use), and it overstates the asymmetry — the 1:1 path at message-router.ts:296-310 does NOT correct state on failure either; it leaves the same persisted-undelivered turn and merely emits a message_send_failed log, so the real delta is observability, not state correctness. Refined to low: this is a deprecated, no-further-investment Linq path (MEMORY.md, ADR-035), the desync is transient and self-healing (cooldown is time-boxed and the counter re-increments on the next inbound), and a raw network throw (not an HTTP error) does correctly hit the catch and skip the update. Real should-fix, but low and overstated, not medium.</sub>
- **[LOW]** Group history query ignores its error; combined with a failed inbound insert it sends an empty messages array to Anthropic (guaranteed 400) — `src/lib/linq/group-bridge.ts:166-198, 231-239`
  processGroupMessage inserts the inbound message at line 166 but only logs on failure and continues (best-effort, consistent with the documented pattern). It then loads history at line 180 destructuring only `data: historyRows` and ignoring the query error. If the inbound insert failed (e.g. transient DB write error or RLS/constraint issue) AND the history SELECT also returns null/errors, `messages` becomes [] and `windowedMessages` (line 198) is empty. The nudge block is skipped because it guards on windowedMessages.length > 0 (line 203), so nothing backfills it. anthropicFetch is then called at line 231 with `messages: []`. The Anthropic Messages API rejects an empty messages array with a 400, which anthropicFetch surfaces as a thrown Error (anthropic.ts:67-69). That throw is caught by the webhook (route.ts:575) — no crash — but the call was guaranteed to fail and burned a round-trip and the 15s timeout window instead of degrading gracefully. Even in the non-empty case, a silent history-load error means Jove answers with truncated/missing context and no signal is logged.
  _→ Capture and log the history query error at group-bridge.ts:180. Guard before the Anthropic call: if windowedMessages.length === 0, log and return { responseText: null } (or fall back to the just-received messageText as a single user turn) rather than calling the API with an empty array. Confident on the mechanism; the empty-array path requires two failures to coincide, hence medium._
  <sub>verified (high): The code chain is real: the inbound insert is best-effort (group-bridge.ts:166-177, logs and continues), the history query destructures only `{ data: historyRows }` and ignores its error (line 180), `historyRows || []` plus `.slice(-30)` yields an empty `windowedMessages` (lines 186-198), the nudge backfill is guarded by `length > 0` so it can't repopulate (line 203), and `messages: windowedMessages` is passed to anthropicFetch with no length guard (lines 231-239), which the Messages API rejects with a 400 that anthropic.ts:67-69 throws and route.ts:575 catches. So the empty-array-to-400 path genuinely exists. But the finding is mis-framed and over-severe: (1) the 'in-flight call after a 200 webhook / burned 15s window' framing is wrong — the Linq webhook processes inline (route.ts:666-667 awaits handleEvent BEFORE returning 200 at line 672), and a 400 is a fast request-validation rejection, not a call that runs to the 15s abort deadline; (2) reaching the empty array requires a low-probability compound — the inbound insert AND the history read both failing on the same service-role (no-RLS) admin client against a just-written table on a conversation with no prior messages, on a deprecating-but-still-live group path. The genuinely valid, currently-present defect is narrower than the headline: line 180 is the only DB op in the file that does not capture/log its error (contrast lines 172, 262, 299), so a partial or transient history-read failure silently truncates Jove's context with zero telemetry. That observability gap is a real Should-fix but low severity, not medium.</sub>

#### Test gaps (9)

- **[HIGH]** In-memory event dedup (seenEvents) has no idempotency test — `src/app/api/linq/webhook/route.ts:654-660`
  Idempotency on event_id is implemented purely in-memory (seenEvents Map) and is untested. Linq retries on non-2xx, so duplicate delivery of the same event_id is the expected case, not the edge case. The branch at route.ts:656-659 must short-circuit a duplicate to 200 ok:true WITHOUT re-invoking handleEvent — otherwise a retried message.received is processed twice (double Jove call, double message insert, double outbound send). Nothing asserts this. Contrast: the Sendblue path tests its DB-backed 23505 dedupe explicitly (route.test.ts:283-295).
  _→ In the new webhook route test, POST the same validly-signed event_id twice in one test invocation (same warm module instance). Assert handleEvent's downstream (mocked handleInboundMessage or the router) is called exactly once and the second response is 200 {ok:true}. Also assert a DIFFERENT event_id is processed (guards against an over-broad key). Note in the test that this only holds within a warm instance — the limitation is real and worth documenting in the assertion comment._
  <sub>verified (high): Confirmed against source. seenEvents is an in-memory Map (route.ts:57) and the dedupe branch (route.ts:656-659) returns 200 {ok:true} without calling handleEvent — it is the ONLY idempotency mechanism (comment at route.ts:54-56 says so explicitly). A re-invoked message.received genuinely double-processes: handleEvent (127-128) -> handleInboundMessage -> processGroupMessage+sendMessage (545-557) or routeInboundMessage (596), and the messages insert at group-bridge.ts:166 has no onConflict/unique constraint, so there is no DB backstop. It is genuinely untested: no test file exists in src/app/api/linq/webhook/ (only route.ts), and no test imports the route or references seenEvents/handleEvent, whereas the Sendblue path tests its 23505 dedupe at webhooks/sendblue/route.test.ts:283. Note: the finding's '[deprecated]' label is wrong — ADR-035 keeps the Linq group path permanently live, so this is active production code, which if anything strengthens the finding. Held at high (test-gap on a retry-driven webhook with real Jove-call/insert/send side effects); the branch logic is correct as written, so the risk is silent regression plus unverified retry/cold-start behavior, which warrants a real integration test rather than my read.</sub>
- **[MED]** verifySignature: no test for signature rejection, replay window, or the seconds-vs-ms timestamp assumption — `src/app/api/linq/webhook/route.ts:86-119`
  verifySignature is the only security boundary on this webhook and has zero test coverage (no test imports the route; grep for verifySignature in *.test.ts returns nothing). Three branches are untested. (1) A valid HMAC over `${timestamp}.${rawBody}` must return true and a tampered body/wrong-secret signature must return 401 at route.ts:634-640. (2) Replay protection at route.ts:97-98 treats the header timestamp as SECONDS (`tsSeconds * 1000`). This is a load-bearing assumption — if Linq sends epoch milliseconds, `Math.abs(Date.now() - tsSeconds*1000)` is astronomically large and EVERY valid request is rejected as stale; if it sends seconds and someone tests with ms, the window never expires. (3) `Number.isNaN(tsSeconds)` (route.ts:96) and the hex-length-mismatch catch (route.ts:115-117) both return false. None of these are pinned. The Sendblue equivalent has a full signature suite (route.test.ts:337-405); Linq has none.
  _→ Add src/app/api/linq/webhook/route.test.ts. Mock crypto-adjacent deps (handleEvent's downstream modules) and set LINQ_WEBHOOK_SECRET. Assert: correct HMAC over `timestamp.rawBody` with a fresh timestamp → handleEvent runs and 200; wrong signature → 401 {error:'invalid signature'} and handleEvent NOT called; timestamp older than MAX_TIMESTAMP_AGE_MS (5min) → 401; non-numeric timestamp → 401; odd-length/invalid-hex signature → 401 (catch path, not a throw). Add an explicit assertion documenting the timestamp UNIT (seconds): a timestamp of `Math.floor(Date.now()/1000)` passes, a timestamp of `Date.now()` (ms) is rejected as stale — this freezes the contract so a future edit can't silently flip it._
  <sub>verified (high): Verified directly: grep for verifySignature and for linq/webhook in *.test.ts both return empty, the webhook dir has only route.ts (no route.test.ts), and verifySignature is the sole 401 gate before handleEvent (route.ts:634-640). The Sendblue contrast is accurate (sendblue/route.test.ts:337-405 has a 4-case sig suite, Linq has none), and Sendblue legitimately has no replay window so the finding does not over-claim parity. Refined to medium rather than high because this is an explicitly deprecated, investment-frozen flow (ADR-035 + MEMORY 'no further investment in Linq'), though still a live security boundary — backfilling vs. retiring is a triage call. - **[MED]** routeInboundMessage internals are entirely untested — the Sendblue suite mocks the whole router — `src/lib/linq/message-router.ts:116-326`

This is a mechanical coverage finding verifiable by grep, hence high confidence.</sub>
- **[MED]** handleCheckpointResponse (text-channel YES/NO/NOT QUITE interception) is untested — `src/lib/linq/message-router.ts:374-534`
  confirmCheckpoint() itself is well covered (confirm-checkpoint.test.ts), but its text-channel driver is not, and the driver owns logic confirmCheckpoint never sees: keyword parsing (YES/Y/CONFIRM, NO/N/DISCARD, NOT QUITE/NOTQUITE/REFINE at lines 384-389), the active-conversation + most-recent-checkpoint lookup (lines 396-414), the `meta.status !== 'pending'` guard (line 419) that prevents re-confirming an already-confirmed/rejected checkpoint, and the NO/NOT-QUITE branches that write checkpoint_meta.status and call insertCheckpointActionMessage (lines 467-506). The status guard at line 419 is an idempotency/data-integrity branch: without it, a second 'YES' after confirmation would re-run confirmCheckpoint. Nothing asserts any of this.
  _→ In message-router.test.ts, drive routeInboundMessage with textContent='YES' and a mocked pending checkpoint: assert confirmCheckpoint called with {messageId, conversationId, userId} and a post-checkpoint processTextMessage(userId, null, convId) follow-up sent. textContent='NO' → messages.update sets checkpoint_meta.status='rejected' and insertCheckpointActionMessage(admin, convId, 'rejected'). textContent='NOT QUITE' → status='refined'. Critical idempotency case: meta.status already 'confirmed' → handleCheckpointResponse returns null (falls through to normal Jove routing), confirmCheckpoint NOT called. Notably the finding's framing as the deprecated Linq group flow is wrong, which makes it worse, not better: routeInboundMessage is the shared 1:1 router imported by the ACTIVE Sendblue webhook (sendblue/route.ts:42; ADR decisions.md:216 routes 1:1 text through Sendblue), and that webhook's test mocks the router out entirely (route.test.ts:67), so nothing exercises this on the live path. I refine severity from high to medium: this is a missing test on code that reads as correct today (the status guard at 419 and the status-write-before-action ordering are present), not a present defect or known-broken path, so it is a should-fix, not a blocker. - **[MED]** processGroupMessage [NO_RESPONSE] handling and non-fatal DB-error branches untested — `src/lib/linq/group-bridge.ts:155-277`

(2) nudgeHint merge (lines 203-219) — must merge into the last user message when it's a user turn, else append a new user turn, to preserve Anthropic's alternating-role requirement; getting this wrong throws a 400 from the API. (3) Message-insert failure (line 172-177) and response-save failure (line 262-267) are intentionally non-fatal (logged, processing continues) — that 'best effort' contract is untested and easy to regress into a throw.

Whether to invest test effort in a deprecated path is a product/priority call for the founder.</sub>

### B · Voice
18 findings — 1 high · 7 med · 10 low
> ⚠️ Confirm whether this flow is live before acting on these.

<details><summary>Flow map</summary>

**Entry points:** `src/components/mobile/ChatInput.tsx (line 5: useVoiceInput hook)`, `src/app/api/voice/token/route.ts (POST endpoint for Deepgram token)`

**Steps:**
1. User initiates voice input — `src/components/mobile/ChatInput.tsx (lines 127-139)`, `src/lib/hooks/useVoiceInput.ts (lines 204-271)`
2. Fetch Deepgram API key from server — `src/lib/hooks/useVoiceInput.ts (lines 90-103)`, `src/app/api/voice/token/route.ts (lines 1-21)`
3. Establish WebSocket connection to Deepgram — `src/lib/hooks/useVoiceInput.ts (lines 105-175)`
4. Start media stream and send audio chunks — `src/lib/hooks/useVoiceInput.ts (lines 204-271)`
5. Update UI with transcript in real-time — `src/lib/hooks/useVoiceInput.ts (lines 143-172)`, `src/components/mobile/ChatInput.tsx (lines 50-55)`
6. Stop recording and finalize transcript — `src/lib/hooks/useVoiceInput.ts (lines 273-296)`, `src/components/mobile/ChatInput.tsx (lines 127-139, 80-102)`
7. Send message to Jove via /api/chat — `src/lib/hooks/useChat.ts (lines 563-650)`, `src/app/api/chat/route.ts (lines 23-100+)`
8. Jove processes transcript as normal user message — `src/lib/persona/call-persona.ts (lines 1-80+)`, `src/lib/persona/system-prompt.ts`, `src/lib/anthropic.ts (lines 45-100)`

**External deps:** Deepgram (Speech-to-Text): wss://api.deepgram.com/v1/listen WebSocket, DEEPGRAM_API_KEY env var · Anthropic API (LLM): POST https://api.anthropic.com/v1/messages, ANTHROPIC_API_KEY env var, claude-opus-4-1-20250805 model · Supabase: Authentication (requireUser), database read (conversations table mode), user context · Vercel: Edge runtime for /api/voice/token route

**Existing tests:** `/Users/jeffwaters/mywalnut/src/lib/hooks/useChat.test.ts: Tests sendMessage(), confirm error messages, checkpoint building (does not directly test voice input hook)`, `/Users/jeffwaters/mywalnut/src/lib/persona/call-persona.test.ts: Tests mapSystemMessages(), wrapPastedContent(), checkpoint detection (indirectly validates message flow)`, `/Users/jeffwaters/mywalnut/src/lib/persona/persona-pipeline.test.ts: Tests conversation context, extraction, checkpoint gates (validates downstream of voice input)`, `No direct test coverage for useVoiceInput.ts hook itself (no .test.ts file found)`

**Notes:** LIVE STATUS: The Voice flow is LIVE and fully integrated. The anchor route /api/voice/token (src/app/api/voice/token/route.ts) is not vestigial — it is an active, minimal auth-gated endpoint that serves the Deepgram API key to authenticated users. KEY DESIGN DECISION: The API key is passed client-side (not a temporary token) because the current Deepgram plan tier does not support temporary token issuance (documented in src/lib/vendors/registry.ts line 119). CRITICAL FLOW: (1) Client requests voice token from /api/voice/token → receives DEEPGRAM_API_KEY (2) Client opens WebSocket to Deepgram with token as subprotocol (3) Client streams audio from MediaRecorder, receives transcription events (4) Interim + final transcripts sync into ChatInput textarea in real-time (5) User submits transcript via normal sendMessage → /api/chat flow, where transcript enters Jove's conversation as an ordinary user message. No special server-side handling for voice; it reaches the same callPersona() pipeline as text input. ARCHITECTURE: Voice input is client-only (WebSocket directly to Deepgram); transcript integration is UI-driven (ChatInput syncs useVoiceInput state to textarea); message sending is unified (transcript → /api/chat with no voice-specific branch). UNTRACED: No tests exist for useVoiceInput.ts itself. The voice → transcript → message flow is validated implicitly through useChat.test.ts and call-persona tests, but no unit test exercises the Deepgram WebSocket lifecycle (connection, reconnect, interim/final handling, cleanup). STATE TOUCHED: Client state (ChatInput: input, recordingState; useVoiceInput: all fields); Server state (Conversation row created/retrieved; messages row inserted; manual_entries potentially created after checkpoint confirmation). NO EXTERNAL STATE MUTATIONS on Deepgram or Anthropic side — both are read-only for voice (Deepgram transcribes; Anthropic responds).

</details>

#### Security (3)

- **[HIGH]** Voice token route hands the long-lived raw DEEPGRAM_API_KEY to the browser — `src/app/api/voice/token/route.ts:20`
  POST /api/voice/token returns the raw account-wide DEEPGRAM_API_KEY in the JSON body ({ key: apiKey }) to any authenticated client. The hook (src/lib/hooks/useVoiceInput.ts:97-98, 116) receives it and puts it directly in the WebSocket subprotocol ["token", key]. This is the actual Deepgram account credential, not a scoped/temporary token. Consequences, all confirmed by the code: (1) The key is trivially extractable by any logged-in user (Network tab, or reading the WS handshake) and is usable directly against api.deepgram.com from anywhere, outside any mywalnut rate limit, until it is manually rotated. The route comment at lines 17-19 asserts "route is auth-protected so only logged-in users can access it" as the mitigation, but anonymous Supabase sessions are first-class in this app (chat route branches on user.is_anonymous at src/app/api/chat/route.ts:84), and requireUser() admits anonymous users, so the gate is weaker than the comment implies. (2) Naming: the variable is tempKeyRef / fetchTempKey (useVoiceInput.ts:35,90) and the route comment says "Temp token generation" — the code names this a temporary token, but it is the permanent account key. That mislabel is how a future reader keeps trusting a control that does not exist. This is the central security exposure of the Voice flow. It does not violate one of the five literal invariants verbatim, but it violates the rules.md "User data... never third-party sharing" posture and the general secret-handling discipline, and it is the kind of leak the security lens exists to catch. Worth noting honestly: the constraint is real — registry.ts:119 and the route comment both record that the current Deepgram plan tier does not support temporary token issuance, and DEEPGRAM_API_KEY is correctly server-only (not NEXT_PUBLIC_, grep-confirmed), so it is not leaked at build time. The exposure is deliberate and documented, but it has no ADR (registry.ts:116 adrRefs: []) and was never weighed as a security decision.
  _→ This is a high-stakes call that should go to a real decision, not a silent ship. Preferred fix: upgrade the Deepgram plan to the tier that issues short-lived scoped tokens (Deepgram's /v1/auth/grant returns ~30s tokens) and return those instead of the account key — this collapses the blast radius from 'rotate the account' to 'token expires in 30s'. If that is not viable now, at minimum: (a) write an ADR that names this as an accepted risk with a rotation cadence and an explicit trigger to revisit; (b) rename tempKeyRef/fetchTempKey/"temp token" comment to say plainly it is the account key, so no future reader trusts a phantom control; (c) consider gating the route to non-anonymous users (auth.user.is_anonymous === false) so a throwaway anon session cannot mint the key. I cannot verify Deepgram's current plan capabilities from here — confirm the token-grant tier with Deepgram before choosing._
  <sub>verified (high): Confirmed in code: src/app/api/voice/token/route.ts:20 returns Response.json({ key: process.env.DEEPGRAM_API_KEY }) — the raw account-wide Deepgram key, not a scoped/temp token — and useVoiceInput.ts:97-98,116 injects it verbatim into the client WebSocket subprotocol to api.deepgram.com, making it trivially extractable (Network tab / WS handshake) and usable directly outside any mywalnut rate limit until manual rotation. The route comment's mitigation ('only logged-in users') is genuinely weaker than asserted: requireUser() (require-user.ts:30) only checks !user and admits anonymous sessions, which are first-class and reachable via signInAnonymously() at SeedScreen.tsx:56 (chat branches on user.is_anonymous at chat/route.ts:84). The 'temp' naming (tempKeyRef/fetchTempKey, route 'Temp token generation' comment) mislabels a permanent credential. Mitigations also hold: key is server-only (grep-confirmed, never NEXT_PUBLIC_, no build-time leak), constraint is real/documented (registry.ts:119), and there is no ADR (registry.ts:116 adrRefs: []) — it was never weighed as a security decision. High, not critical, because the exposed credential is Deepgram's transcription account (quota/billing abuse, eavesdropping on Deepgram usage), not a Supabase service-role key or direct user data; the true abuse ceiling depends on Deepgram-side spend caps/scopes, which only a human checking the Deepgram dashboard can confirm.</sub>
- **[LOW]** Auth-gated route returns a raw long-lived vendor key on every call with no rate limit or ADR — out of test-gap scope, flagged for security review — `src/app/api/voice/token/route.ts:20`
  This is a security-design observation, not a missing-test finding (the route calls no Anthropic API, so the rate-limiting Security Rule does not apply). An authenticated user can pull the raw DEEPGRAM_API_KEY on every request, and there is no ADR for the client-side-key decision (registry.ts:116 adrRefs is empty). Recommend a real human security/architecture review of the client-side-key tradeoff and whether to rate-limit or rotate the key; do not file this as a unit-test ticket.
  _→ Route to security/architecture review: decide whether key issuance should be rate-limited and whether an ADR should document the client-side-key tradeoff per the vendor registry note._
  <sub>verified (high): Verified at src/app/api/voice/token/route.ts:20: the route returns the raw long-lived DEEPGRAM_API_KEY (line 9) to any authenticated caller, gated only by requireUser() (a bare supabase.auth.getUser() check) with no rate limiter — grep across src/app/api/voice/ and rate-limit.ts confirms no voice limiter exists — and the client hook hands that exact key to a browser Deepgram WebSocket (useVoiceInput.ts:116). The finding's framing is accurate: this route calls no Anthropic API, so the rate-limiting Security Rule does not technically apply, and there is genuinely no ADR for the client-side-key decision (registry.ts:116 adrRefs is empty; grep -ic deepgram docs/decisions.md returns 0; state.md:88 independently notes 'no ADR yet'). Low severity is correctly calibrated — exposure is gated behind authentication and Deepgram's token subprotocol expects a client-presentable key — and the recommendation to route this to a human security/architecture review rather than a unit-test ticket is the right call.</sub>
- **[LOW]** Voice token route has no rate limiting, so the key-minting endpoint can be drained in a loop — `src/app/api/voice/token/route.ts:5-21`
  POST /api/voice/token has no rate limiter (grep-confirmed: no rate-limit import in src/app/api/voice/, and the route body calls only requireUser() then returns the key). The literal invariant 'routes that call the Anthropic API have rate limiting' does not bite — this route calls Deepgram, not Anthropic — so this is not a verbatim invariant violation. But combined with finding #1 it is the same exposure with no throttle: any authenticated (including anonymous) session can POST this endpoint in a tight loop to repeatedly exfiltrate the account key, and there is no per-user cap or signal. Every other sensitive route in the app is rate-limited (src/lib/rate-limit.ts:60-86 defines limiters for chat, session-summary, checkpoint-confirm, manual-edit, OTP send/verify, waitlist) — the key-issuing voice route is the conspicuous omission. Note the rate-limit module fails open in dev/preview and throws at module load only in VERCEL_ENV=production (rate-limit.ts:34-43), and ADR-038/state.md record that Upstash prod env vars have historically been unprovisioned — so even an added limiter is only as real as the Upstash provisioning.
  _→ Add a dedicated limiter (e.g. voiceTokenHour = makeLimiter(...) keyed by user.id) in src/lib/rate-limit.ts and call checkLimit in the route before returning the key. Lower urgency than #1 because if #1 is fixed by short-lived tokens, repeated minting matters far less. Confirm Upstash prod env vars are actually set (ADR-038 / state.md In-Flight flags them as a historical gap) — a limiter that fails open is not a control._
  <sub>verified (high): Confirmed: src/app/api/voice/token/route.ts:5-21 calls requireUser() then returns the raw DEEPGRAM_API_KEY with no rate-limit import or call anywhere in src/app/api/voice/ (grep clean), while every other sensitive route uses the limiters defined at rate-limit.ts:60-86; requireUser() (require-user.ts:30) admits anonymous sessions (proven real by chat/route.ts:84 handling user.is_anonymous), so the loop-drain path is genuinely reachable. The gap is real and currently present, but I refine medium down to low: the route hands the long-lived account key to the browser by design (comment at lines 17-19 admits temp-token minting was skipped), so any authenticated client already has the key without looping — a rate limiter is cheap defense-in-depth on fresh acquisition but does not address the key-to-browser exposure that is the actual risk. The broader key-scope/rotation question warrants a human ops review.</sub>

#### Conflicts (2)

- **[MED]** Stale-closure in WebSocket onclose disables the reconnect on the first recording session — `src/lib/hooks/useVoiceInput.ts:134`
  connectWebSocket() is a non-memoized function recreated each render; its ws.onclose handler (line 128-141) closes over the render-time value of recordingState. In startRecording() the WebSocket is created at line 243, but setRecordingState('recording') does not run until line 266 — so the onclose closure captures recordingState === 'idle'. The reconnect guard at line 134 (`recordingState === 'recording'`) therefore evaluates false when Deepgram drops the socket mid-session, and handleReconnect() never fires. The advertised 'one silent reconnect for mid-session drops' is dead on the first recording. (It can only work if a later render re-creates the closure with 'recording' captured AND that newer ws replaces wsRef, which the current code never does — handlers are bound once at WS creation.) recordingState should be mirrored into a ref read inside onclose, the same pattern already used for isStoppingRef and reconnectAttemptedRef in this file. Confidence: high on the mechanism; the stale capture is unambiguous from the call ordering.
  _→ Mirror recordingState into a recordingStateRef (set alongside every setRecordingState call) and read recordingStateRef.current inside ws.onclose instead of the captured recordingState. Add a hook test that opens the WS, dispatches onclose with isStoppingRef=false, and asserts handleReconnect runs._
  <sub>verified (high): Real bug. `connectWebSocket` (src/lib/hooks/useVoiceInput.ts:105) is non-memoized and its `ws.onclose` (lines 128-141) lexically captures `recordingState`. It is invoked from `startRecording` at line 243, which is a useCallback memoized on `[micPermission]` only (line 271) and sets `setRecordingState('recording')` at line 266 — AFTER the WS and its handler are created. So the closure captures `recordingState === 'idle'`, and the onclose reconnect guard at line 134 (`recordingState === 'recording'`) is false on a mid-session Deepgram drop, so `handleReconnect()` never fires. onclose handlers bind once and wsRef is never replaced by a render holding 'recording'. The sibling guards (isStoppingRef line 130, reconnectAttemptedRef line 135, tempKeyRef line 136) all use live refs and would permit the reconnect — line 134's stale state read is the sole blocker, confirming the inconsistency. No `recordingStateRef` mirror exists anywhere in the codebase, so the proposed fix (mirror state into a ref read inside onclose, matching the existing isStoppingRef/reconnectAttemptedRef pattern) is genuinely absent. I keep severity at medium/should-fix, not a blocker: it only degrades an advertised resilience feature (the 'one silent reconnect' comment at line 132) on the rare mid-session-drop path; happy path and stopRecording (live isStoppingRef) are unaffected, and no data loss or security exposure results. One refinement: the 'dead on the first recording' framing understates it — because recordingState is always 'idle' when the closure is captured inside startRecording, the reconnect is effectively dead on every recording session, not just the first.</sub>
- **[MED]** startRecording has no re-entrancy guard — a second tap during async setup orphans a mic stream and WebSocket — `src/lib/hooks/useVoiceInput.ts:204`
  startRecording() awaits getUserMedia (216), fetchTempKey (232, a network round-trip to the Edge route), and connectWebSocket (243) before setRecordingState('recording') at line 266. For that whole window recordingState stays 'idle', so ChatInput's getButtonMode() returns 'mic' (ChatInput.tsx:144-149) and the mic button stays tappable (its disabled attr is only `disabled && buttonMode==='mic'`, ChatInput.tsx:373). A double-tap, or an impatient second tap during a slow fetchTempKey, invokes startRecording twice concurrently. The second invocation overwrites streamRef/wsRef/mediaRecorderRef (229,251,257), orphaning the first MediaStream, WebSocket, and MediaRecorder. stopRecording()/cleanupAll() only act on the current ref values, so the orphaned stream keeps the mic live and the orphaned WebSocket keeps streaming audio to Deepgram (consuming the shared API key) until page unload. Confidence: high on the mechanics; realism is moderate and rises with fetchTempKey latency.
  _→ Add an isStartingRef guard: set it true at the top of startRecording, bail if already true/recording, clear it in a finally. Alternatively flip recordingState to a 'connecting' state immediately on entry so the button leaves 'mic' mode and disables. Test: invoke startRecording twice without awaiting and assert only one stream/WS/recorder is created._
  <sub>verified (high): Confirmed in code. startRecording (useVoiceInput.ts:204) awaits getUserMedia (216), fetchTempKey (232 — a POST to /api/voice/token that does a requireUser Supabase auth round-trip, so a real network window), and connectWebSocket (243) before setRecordingState('recording') at 266; throughout that window recordingState is 'idle', so getButtonMode() returns 'mic' (ChatInput.tsx, actual path src/components/mobile/ChatInput.tsx:144-149) and the button's disabled is only `disabled && buttonMode==='mic'` (line 373), leaving it tappable. There is no re-entrancy guard (isStoppingRef only guards stop), so a second tap re-enters handleMicToggle's else branch (line 137) and runs startRecording concurrently, overwriting streamRef/wsRef/mediaRecorderRef (229,251,257); stopRecording/cleanupAll act only on current refs, so the orphaned MediaStream keeps the mic live and the orphaned WebSocket keeps streaming to Deepgram on the shared DEEPGRAM_API_KEY (route.ts:20 returns the raw account key) until unload. Medium is right: real privacy + shared-quota consequence, but gated behind a double-tap in a bounded window and self-heals on unmount; for a returning user with mic already 'granted', getUserMedia resolves promptlessly so the fetchTempKey/WS latency is the sole exposed gap, matching the finding's 'realism rises with latency' caveat.</sub>

#### Failure paths (4)

- **[MED]** Mid-session WebSocket drop never reconnects: onclose reads a stale recordingState closure that is always "idle" — `src/lib/hooks/useVoiceInput.ts:128-141`
  connectWebSocket() assigns ws.onclose once, during the await in startRecording (line 243), BEFORE setRecordingState("recording") runs (line 266). startRecording is a useCallback with deps [micPermission], so the connectWebSocket closure captures the recordingState value from that render — which is "idle" at the moment the socket is established. The reconnect guard at line 134 (`recordingState === "recording"`) therefore reads "idle" when Deepgram drops the connection mid-session, so handleReconnect() is never called. The documented "one silent reconnect for mid-session drops" is dead code in practice: a real network blip during recording silently kills transcription with no error toast and no recovery — audio keeps being captured by MediaRecorder but ws.send() at line 260 no-ops because readyState is no longer OPEN, so the user keeps talking and loses everything they say after the drop. This is a behavioral defect, not a design opinion: the feature was built to reconnect and does not. I am confident on the mechanism (closure ordering is unambiguous); I am inferring the exact UX from how Deepgram closes idle/dropped sockets. The fix is to gate on a ref (e.g. isStoppingRef is already a ref and works correctly) rather than the recordingState state value — read an isRecordingRef.current instead of the captured recordingState.
  _→ Replace the `recordingState === "recording"` check at line 134 with a ref (add an isRecordingRef set true in startRecording, false in stopRecording) so onclose sees live state. Add a unit test for useVoiceInput that mocks WebSocket: start recording, fire onclose without setting isStoppingRef, assert connectWebSocket is called a second time._
  <sub>verified (high): Confirmed real. `connectWebSocket` (a plain function recreated each render) wires `ws.onclose` at line 128, capturing `recordingState` from the render in which it runs. The live socket's `onclose` is always created at line 243 inside `startRecording`, which executes strictly before `setRecordingState("recording")` at line 266, so the captured value is always "idle" — the guard `recordingState === "recording"` at line 134 can never be true for a mid-session drop, and `handleReconnect()` never fires. `onclose` is assigned exactly once per socket and never reassigned (verified by grep), so no later render rebinds it on the open socket; no path captures "recording". Downstream is as claimed: no reconnect, no `stopRecording()`, no error toast, button stays in "stop" mode (ChatInput.tsx:145), MediaRecorder keeps firing but `ws.send()` no-ops on the closed socket (line 260 readyState guard), so post-drop speech is silently lost. The "one silent reconnect" feature is effectively dead code. Fix shape is correct: gate on a live `isRecordingRef.current` ref (the sibling `isStoppingRef` at line 36/130 already proves refs read correctly here) instead of the captured state. Severity medium not high: real silent-loss path with a clean behavior-preserving fix, but triggered only by an intermittent external socket drop, not every session, and it loses transient voice transcription rather than corrupting stored data or breaching security. The exact UX is an inference on Deepgram's close behavior (correctly flagged); the closure mechanism itself is unambiguous.</sub>
- **[LOW]** Voice token route hands out a live Deepgram API key with no rate limiting — `src/app/api/voice/token/route.ts:5-21`
  POST /api/voice/token returns the raw DEEPGRAM_API_KEY (not a scoped temp token — by design per registry.ts:119) to any authenticated user, gated only by requireUser(). There is no rate limiter on this route (confirmed: no rate-limit import, and it is absent from the list of routes that import checkLimits). CLAUDE.md's Security Rule "All routes that call the Anthropic API must have rate limiting" does not literally apply (this route calls no LLM), so this is not a hard-rule violation. But the route is the single distribution point for a long-lived vendor credential: an authenticated client can poll it freely and exfiltrate the key, and there is no per-user ceiling on how often the key is dispensed. The auth gate is correct and the env-missing 503 path (lines 10-15) is handled cleanly. fetchTempKey on the client (useVoiceInput.ts:90-103) handles non-ok and network failure correctly, so the client side of this failure path is sound. Confidence: high that there is no limiter; the severity is low because the route is auth-only and the key is already shipped to the browser by design. Whether the plan-tier constraint that forces a raw key is acceptable is a vendor/security judgment for a human — this is the kind of credential-handling call worth a real security review rather than my read.
  _→ Add a lightweight per-user limiter (reuse the Upstash chatAuthMinute pattern) to /api/voice/token to cap key dispensing, and track an ADR for the raw-key decision (registry.ts notes there is none). Flag for human review whether passing the long-lived key to the client is acceptable for launch vs. upgrading the Deepgram tier to scoped tokens._
  <sub>verified (high): Verified directly: src/app/api/voice/token/route.ts:5-21 returns the raw DEEPGRAM_API_KEY (Response.json({ key: apiKey })) gated only by requireUser() (auth check only, require-user.ts:30), with no rate-limit import — the only checkLimits consumers are src/app/api/chat/route.ts and its test, and src/middleware.ts excludes non-admin /api routes (matcher api(?!/admin), line 97) so nothing blankets it. The 'raw key by design' note is confirmed at registry.ts:119 (plan tier lacks temp-token issuance), and the client at src/lib/hooks/useVoiceInput.ts:90-103 handles !res.ok and the catch path correctly. So the issue is real and currently present: an authenticated user can poll the single distribution point for a long-lived credential with no per-user ceiling. Severity is correctly low — it is not a hard-rule violation (route calls no LLM), the key is already shipped to the browser by design, and a limiter only caps handout frequency rather than closing exfiltration, so 'add a rate limiter' is defense-in-depth while the real fix (temp tokens on plan upgrade) is a vendor/security call the finding rightly defers to a human.</sub>
- **[LOW]** Typing while recording calls stopRecording but does not clear the transcript ref, so the next recording can be polluted — `src/components/mobile/ChatInput.tsx:111-117, src/lib/hooks/useVoiceInput.ts:273-296`
  handleInput (ChatInput.tsx:113-115) calls voice.stopRecording() when the user types mid-recording, but unlike handleSend (line 87) and handleMicToggle, it does not call voice.clearTranscript(). stopRecording (useVoiceInput.ts:273-296) does not reset finalTranscriptRef — only startRecording (line 267) and clearTranscript (line 199) do. Sequence: user records partial speech, taps into the textarea and edits, then taps the mic again. startRecording does reset finalTranscriptRef to "" at line 267, so the stale ref is cleared on the next start — meaning the practical blast radius is limited. The concrete defect is narrower: between the type-interruption and the next start, finalTranscriptRef still holds the old transcript, and the sync effect (ChatInput.tsx:51-55) only writes while isRecording is true, so it does not clobber the user's typing. Net: this is a latent inconsistency (stopRecording leaves transcript state non-empty while recordingState is idle) rather than a user-visible data loss today. Confidence: high on the code paths; I verified startRecording's reset neutralizes the worst case. Worth tracking because the three stop sites handle transcript cleanup inconsistently and a future change to the sync effect could turn this into visible pollution.
  _→ Make stopRecording the single place that decides transcript fate, or have handleInput call voice.clearTranscript() for symmetry with handleSend/handleMicToggle. Add a test asserting that after stopRecording the transcript/finalTranscriptRef state is in a defined state, and that a type-interrupt followed by a new recording does not prepend old text._
  <sub>verified (high): Verified against the code: stopRecording (useVoiceInput.ts:273-296) never resets finalTranscriptRef or calls setTranscript(""), and handleInput (ChatInput.tsx:111-117) calls voice.stopRecording() without clearTranscript() — unlike handleSend (line 86-87) which calls both. So after a type-interruption, finalTranscriptRef and the transcript state stay non-empty while recordingState is "idle": a genuine latent inconsistency, and the three stop sites do handle cleanup inconsistently. But the finding correctly rates it low: there is no user-visible pollution today because the sync effect (ChatInput.tsx:51-55) is gated on isRecording, and every recording restart funnels through startRecording (line 267-269), which resets finalTranscriptRef = "" and setTranscript("") synchronously before any new Deepgram message can accumulate onto a stale ref. I confirmed ChatInput is the only consumer of the hook (grep), so no other re-entry path bypasses that reset. Real, narrowly scoped, severity correct.</sub>
- **[LOW]** No test that a token-fetch failure stops the already-acquired mic stream (leak path) — `src/lib/hooks/useVoiceInput.ts:232-237`
  After getUserMedia succeeds, if fetchTempKey() returns null, startRecording must stop the live stream tracks and clear streamRef. If this regresses, the microphone stays active after a token failure. Untested.
