  <sub>verified (high): Factually accurate but severity is overstated. The cleanup logic at src/lib/hooks/useVoiceInput.ts:233-236 is present and correct today — on a null key from fetchTempKey() it calls stream.getTracks().forEach(t => t.stop()) and sets streamRef.current = null — so there is no current leak. And it is genuinely untested: no test file imports useVoiceInput, and grep for fetchTempKey/getUserMedia/streamRef/MediaRecorder across all 46 test files returns nothing (the three 'voice' matches are unrelated — a manual-entry name in useChat.test.ts and Jove VOICE_RULES prompt content in system-prompt.test.ts). But this is a 'missing test for a hypothetical future regression on already-correct code,' which is a Consider, not a Should-fix: the entire 308-line hook has zero coverage (including the equally-relevant WebSocket-failure cleanup at 247-248 and the unmount cleanupAll), so singling out lines 232-237 is arbitrary, and no documented discipline in CLAUDE.md or system.md mandates branch coverage on browser-only hooks — the repo's hook-test pattern (useChat.test.ts) only unit-tests extracted pure helpers, and no jsdom/mocked-navigator harness exists to test this path. Real gap, honestly the leak claim is conditional on a future edit, so medium overstates it.</sub>

Line 119 (ws.onopen) does reset reconnectAttemptedRef.current = false, and it is the only thing that re-arms the one-shot guard at line 135 mid-session — without it, a second drop in the same recording session would hit the guard (set true at line 138) and never reconnect. And no test exercises this: grep for useVoiceInput across all .test.ts/.tsx returns NONE, while the repo does test hooks via vitest (useChat.test.ts, usePersonaDyslexicFont.test.ts) and voice is a live feature (wired into ChatInput.tsx, /api/voice/token route exists), not a Dead Feature. So the gap is genuinely present. I refine severity from medium to low: the project's actual discipline is targeted regression tests of extracted pure helpers (useChat.test.ts only tests buildChatMessageFromEvent; the sibling hook's own WebSocket/stream lifecycle is itself untested), there is no documented rule mandating coverage of WS lifecycle or ref-reset logic, and the handlers here are ref-closures not extracted pure functions. It is a real but minor untested branch in a low-blast-radius client feature, consistent with the codebase being light on client-side lifecycle tests — a Consider, not a should-fix-soon. (Separately and out of scope: the onclose closure captures recordingState at line 134 from a stale render, a latent correctness hazard a test alone would not fix — noting so the low severity is not read as a clean bill of health on the reconnect path.)</sub>

### B · Waitlist & public landing
17 findings — 2 high · 6 med · 9 low

<details><summary>Flow map</summary>

**Entry points:** `src/middleware.ts (lines 59-83)`, `src/app/page.tsx (landing root)`, `src/app/waitlist/page.tsx (early-access/waitlist)`

**Steps:**
1. Middleware routing + auth check — `src/middleware.ts`
2. Load and render public landing page — `src/app/layout.tsx`, `src/app/page.tsx`, `src/components/landing/RevealInit.tsx`, `src/components/landing/HeroRotator.tsx`
3. User fills ContactForm at / or WaitlistForm at /waitlist — `src/app/page.tsx`, `src/app/waitlist/page.tsx`, `src/components/landing/ContactForm.tsx`, `src/components/shared/WaitlistForm.tsx`
4. Validate and rate-limit request on API — `src/app/api/waitlist/route.ts`, `src/lib/rate-limit.ts`
5. Parse and validate form body — `src/app/api/waitlist/route.ts`
6. Query and deduplicate via admin client — `src/app/api/waitlist/route.ts`, `src/lib/supabase/admin.ts`
7. Insert new row or handle race condition — `src/app/api/waitlist/route.ts`
8. Frontend receives response and updates state — `src/components/landing/ContactForm.tsx`, `src/components/shared/WaitlistForm.tsx`
9. User clicks Sign In and attempts signup (OAuth or email) — `src/app/api/auth/signup/route.ts`, `src/lib/beta-allowlist.ts`
10. User completes email confirmation link — `src/app/auth/callback/route.ts`
11. Authenticated user accesses /app — `src/middleware.ts`, `src/app/app/page.tsx`

**External deps:** Supabase (NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL, NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY, SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY): Auth exchange, session, signup, admin delete; tables: waitlist, beta_allowlist, auth.users · Upstash Redis (@upstash/ratelimit, @upstash/redis, UPSTASH_REDIS_REST_URL, UPSTASH_REDIS_REST_TOKEN): Sliding window rate limiting (waitlistSubmitHour 3/hour) · Google OAuth via Supabase: Redirect URL allowlist must include /auth/callback · PostHog: Analytics provider, loaded via PostHogProvider in layout.tsx · Next.js: Middleware, server/client components, dynamic rendering (force-dynamic), image optimization

**Existing tests:** `src/app/api/waitlist/route.test.ts (lines 1-184): Tests rate limit, IP extraction, JSON validation, email validation, dedup, insert success, source truncation, unique constraint race (code 23505), already_listed response. Mocks: createAdminClient, checkLimit.`, `src/lib/rate-limit.test.ts: Tests Upstash Redis limiter behavior`

**Notes:** ENTRY POINTS: (1) src/middleware.ts handles all routing decisions and auth checks. (2) src/app/page.tsx is the public landing at /. (3) src/app/waitlist/page.tsx is the early-access/waitlist form page (reads query param reason=not_allowlisted). STATE TOUCHED: Browser localStorage (mywalnut.theme, mywalnut.persona-dyslexic), Supabase auth cookies (sb-*-auth-token, sb-*-auth-token-code-verifier), React form state (ContactForm/WaitlistForm: email, source, state machine), Supabase auth session (user object from getUser()/exchangeCodeForSession). CRITICAL FLOWS: (A) Public landing → ContactForm at anchor → POST /api/waitlist (rate-limited 3/hour by IP, deduped, inserted to waitlist table). (B) Waitlist visitor → WaitlistForm → same API. (C) Signup (email or OAuth) requires beta_allowlist entry; OAuth signup is post-validated in /auth/callback (new users only, fails closed). Database: waitlist table (email PK unique, source text|null, status defaults 'waiting'), beta_allowlist table (email PK, used as gate for signup). Rate limit: Upstash Redis sliding window, 3/hour per IP for waitlist; fails open in dev/preview if env vars missing, throws in production. Test coverage: waitlist route thoroughly tested (rate limit, validation, dedup, race); auth routes not found as dedicated tests.

</details>

#### Security (2)

- **[HIGH]** Anon RLS INSERT policy lets browsers write to waitlist directly, bypassing the API route's rate limit and validation — `supabase/migrations/20260417000000_squash_baseline.sql:947`
  The waitlist table has `CREATE POLICY waitlist_anon_insert ... FOR INSERT TO anon WITH CHECK (true)` plus `GRANT ALL ON TABLE public.waitlist TO anon` (line 1108). The anon key is shipped to every browser via NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY (src/lib/supabase/client.ts:4-6). So any anonymous visitor can run `supabase.from('waitlist').insert({email, source})` directly from the client and never touch /api/waitlist. That bypasses the ONLY abuse gate the route documents as its purpose: the IP rate limit (route.ts:28, waitlistSubmitHour 3/hr), the email format check (route.ts:50-56 isValidEmail), and the MAX_SOURCE_LENGTH=500 truncation (route.ts:58-59). The DB CHECK constraints only enforce email is lowercase/trimmed and status is in an enum — they do NOT enforce email format and do NOT bound `source` length, so an attacker can insert unlimited rows with multi-megabyte `source` payloads at the anon key's native rate, using the table as free storage and flooding the admin waitlist view. The route comment at route.ts:62-64 reasons only about the ABSENCE of an anon SELECT policy (correctly preventing enumeration) and never accounts for the anon INSERT policy that makes the server-side route optional. This is the security defect in this flow: the route's protections are real but not the only write path.
  _→ Drop the waitlist_anon_insert policy (and revoke INSERT from anon) so the table is service-role-write-only, matching beta_allowlist which is fully default-deny to anon and only written via the admin client. All legitimate writes already go through /api/waitlist with the admin client (route.ts:82), so removing the anon path is behavior-preserving for the app while closing the bypass. If a direct-from-browser insert is ever intentionally wanted, it must carry its own rate limit and length/format checks at the DB layer (CHECK on length(source), a stricter email constraint) — but the simpler and safer move is to delete the policy. VERIFY before shipping the fix: confirm no client-side code path inserts into waitlist directly (grep already shows the only insert is the admin client in route.ts:82; the browser client is used only for auth). This is a schema/RLS change — apply via migration per ADR-036, and a real human should confirm the prod policy matches the migration, since RLS drift between dashboard and repo has bitten this project before._
  <sub>verified (high): Verified every claim against the code. `supabase/migrations/20260417000000_squash_baseline.sql:947` defines `CREATE POLICY waitlist_anon_insert ON public.waitlist FOR INSERT TO anon WITH CHECK (true)` and line 1108 grants `GRANT ALL ON TABLE public.waitlist TO anon`; with RLS enabled (line 941) an anon caller needs both, and both are present, so a browser holding `NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY` (src/lib/supabase/client.ts:4-6) can run `supabase.from('waitlist').insert(...)` directly. Both real client forms (WaitlistForm.tsx:26, ContactForm.tsx:37) go through `fetch('/api/waitlist')`, so the anon INSERT policy is a genuine parallel write path the app never uses, and it bypasses the route's only abuse gates: the IP rate limit (route.ts:28), isValidEmail (route.ts:50-56), and the MAX_SOURCE_LENGTH=500 cap (route.ts:14,59). The table's only CHECK constraints (migration lines 348-349) enforce email-lowercase/trimmed and the status enum — they do not validate email format or bound `source` length, so unbounded multi-megabyte `source` rows are insertable at the anon key's native rate; the enum even permits an attacker to self-set status='invited'. The route comment at route.ts:62-64 reasons only about the absent anon SELECT policy and never accounts for the anon INSERT policy, which is the exact false confidence flagged. No later migration drops the policy or revokes the grant, and no ADR/doc documents it as intentional. High severity is appropriate (trivially exploitable unauthenticated write-amplification/spam/free-storage that defeats the route's documented purpose), though I'd hold at high and not above: it is not read-exfiltration or auth bypass — the deliberate no-SELECT-policy choice still prevents enumeration, and dedupe/reads remain server-side. Confirming the exploit fires in prod (vs. local, where the squash is marked already-applied) would benefit from a real anon-key insert test, but the policy+grant+RLS combination is unambiguous from the schema.</sub>
- **[LOW]** Unauthenticated /api/auth/signup has no rate limit; can be used to trigger Supabase auth emails for any allowlisted address — `src/app/api/auth/signup/route.ts:12`
  POST /api/auth/signup is public and unthrottled. It calls isEmailAllowlisted (a DB read via admin client) and then supabase.auth.signUp, which causes Supabase to send a confirmation email. There is no checkLimit call, unlike the comparable public/abuse-sensitive routes that ARE keyed and limited (phone OTP send at src/app/api/user/phone/route.ts:98, OTP verify at verify/route.ts:50, and the waitlist route at route.ts:28). NOTE on the hard rule: the documented invariant is 'all routes that call the Anthropic API must have rate limiting.' This route does NOT call Anthropic (confirmed by grep across the flow), so this is NOT a hard-rule violation — it is an inference, flagged at low severity. The practical exposure is bounded: the allowlist gate (route.ts:30-33, fails closed) means only emails already on beta_allowlist can reach signUp, so this is not an open email-bomb vector against arbitrary addresses. Residual risk: an attacker who knows or guesses an allowlisted email could repeatedly trigger Supabase confirmation emails to that person, and Supabase's own per-project email rate limits become the only backstop.
  _→ Add an IP-keyed limiter (e.g. a new authSignupHour makeLimiter, modeled on waitlistSubmitHour in src/lib/rate-limit.ts:86) and call checkLimit at the top of the handler before the allowlist lookup, returning rateLimitedResponse on failure. This is a product/ops judgment call, not a blocker — the allowlist already caps the blast radius. Recommend tracking rather than gating this ship._
  <sub>verified (high): Confirmed in code: POST /api/auth/signup (src/app/api/auth/signup/route.ts:12-51) is public and has no checkLimit call; it goes straight to isEmailAllowlisted then supabase.auth.signUp (line 38), which sends a Supabase confirmation email. The middleware excludes all non-admin API routes (src/middleware.ts:97, "API routes self-gate"), so nothing throttles it upstream. By contrast the comparable public/abuse-sensitive routes ARE limited — waitlist (waitlist/route.ts:28, IP-keyed), phone OTP send (user/phone/route.ts:98) and verify (verify/route.ts:50, phone-keyed) — and the reusable limiter infra exists in src/lib/rate-limit.ts. The finding's scoping is accurate: not a hard-rule violation (no Anthropic call), and the allowlist fails closed (beta-allowlist.ts:24-41 returns false on error/no-match), so the blast radius is bounded to already-allowlisted addresses rather than arbitrary email-bombing. Real, currently-present, but correctly rated low: a harassment/email-amplification vector against a specific allowlisted victim with Supabase's project email limits as the only backstop. Fix is to add an IP- (and ideally email-) keyed checkLimit before signUp, mirroring the waitlist route.</sub>

#### Conflicts (3)

- **[MED]** OAuth allowlist gate fails open when deleteUser fails and the user retries past the 60s window — `src/app/auth/callback/route.ts:74-115`
  The beta gate for Google OAuth signup lives only here, and only fires while the user is "new" (created within NEW_USER_WINDOW_MS = 60_000). The flow: exchangeCodeForSession creates the auth.users row and sets session cookies, then isEmailAllowlisted returns false, then admin.auth.admin.deleteUser(user.id) is attempted. That delete is wrapped in try/catch (line 85-93) and on failure the code only logs and proceeds to bounce the user to /waitlist with cookies cleared — the auth.users row is NOT removed. The user now exists permanently. On any subsequent OAuth attempt the user is a RETURNING user (no new row), so created_at is unchanged and ageMs grows. If the retry happens more than 60s after the original row was created, isNewUser is false (line 77), the entire allowlist block is skipped, exchangeCodeForSession succeeds, and the non-allowlisted user gets a valid session and lands in /app. There is no secondary allowlist check anywhere downstream: middleware (src/middleware.ts:71-81) only checks that a user exists, and /app (src/app/app/page.tsx:13-22) deliberately performs no getUser() and no allowlist check. So a single transient deleteUser failure plus a >60s retry yields permanent unauthorized beta access. This is a contradictory-guardrail / idempotency gap: the gate assumes delete always succeeds and that a blocked user is always still inside the new-user window on retry; neither is guaranteed.
  _→ Add a test for auth/callback (none exists today) covering: (a) deleteUser throws -> user is still bounced AND a durable block is recorded; (b) a non-allowlisted RETURNING user (ageMs > 60s) hitting the callback is still denied. Fix options: re-run isEmailAllowlisted for ALL users (not only new ones) and sign out + redirect when not allowlisted regardless of account age — the cost is one extra allowlist read on every login, which is cheap and closes the window; OR, if the new-user fast path must stay, make the delete failure fail closed (do not clear cookies and bounce silently — instead retry the delete or flag the row for cleanup and still deny the session). Recommend a human review on the auth flow before changing it, since exchangeCodeForSession cookie handling here is delicate (the comments at lines 29-31 warn about losing cookies)._
  <sub>verified (high): Confirmed in code: the OAuth beta gate is the only allowlist check on the Google path, it fires only while isNewUser is true (ageMs < NEW_USER_WINDOW_MS = 60_000, route.ts:77), and the deleteUser failure path (route.ts:85-93) only logs and falls through — the auth.users row persists. A reference search confirms isEmailAllowlisted is called nowhere downstream (middleware.ts:77-81 and app/page.tsx only check that a user exists), so once a session is valid nothing re-verifies allowlist membership. On a >60s retry the persisted orphan re-authenticates as a returning user with its original created_at, isNewUser is false, the entire gate block is skipped, and the user lands in /app — a genuine fail-open in an access gate. The one link I cannot confirm purely from code is Supabase/GoTrue row-reuse semantics (that a re-OAuth of an existing identity reuses the same row and original created_at rather than minting a new one); this is the standard, expected GoTrue behavior and is exactly what the route's own created_at-window design already assumes, so the bug direction holds — but it warrants a real integration test before trusting blind. Refining high → medium: it requires a transient deleteUser failure (an attacker cannot force it) plus a specific retry timing, and the blast radius is unauthorized entry to a closed beta, not data loss or cross-user exposure. Still a real should-fix: the gate just went live (middleware was dormant until commit 7a0f1f8, 2026-05-29, edge gating newly enforced) and has no test covering this path. Note the deeper root cause — a 60s time window as the sole discriminator means even a legitimate first exchange delayed past 60s (slow consent screen, clock skew) would skip the gate with no orphan required.</sub>
- **[LOW]** Google OAuth button does not set loading, allowing concurrent signInWithOAuth calls — `src/components/onboarding/LoginScreen.tsx:52-63`
  handleGoogle (line 52) never calls setLoading(true), unlike handleSubmit (line 35), handleSignup (line 69) and handleForgotSubmit (line 99) which all set loading and disable their submit buttons. The Google button's disabled state is bound to `loading` (line 410: disabled={loading}), but since handleGoogle never sets it, the button stays enabled across the entire redirect handshake. supabase.auth.signInWithOAuth triggers a full-page navigation to Google, so in the common case the navigation moots a second click — but if the OAuth provider is slow to redirect, or the network stalls, a user can click "Continue with Google" repeatedly, firing multiple signInWithOAuth calls. Each call regenerates the PKCE code-verifier cookie (sb-*-auth-token-code-verifier); the last write wins, so an earlier in-flight authorize request can come back with a code that no longer matches the stored verifier, causing exchangeCodeForSession at auth/callback/route.ts:57 to fail and bounce the user to /login?error=reset_link_expired (a misleading message for an OAuth flow). This is a client/server state-desync from a double-submit that the other three handlers already guard against.
  _→ Set loading state in handleGoogle the same way the other handlers do (set true before signInWithOAuth, clear it in a finally or on the error branch), so the button disables on first click. Low-risk, mechanical, and matches the existing pattern in the same file._
  <sub>verified (high): Confirmed in code: handleGoogle (LoginScreen.tsx:52-63) never calls setLoading(true) and has no try/finally, while handleSubmit (35), handleSignup (69), and handleForgotSubmit (99) all do; the Google button is disabled={loading} (line 410) so it never disables, leaving it clickable across the OAuth handshake. The PKCE code-verifier-overwrite consequence is mechanically valid and a failed exchangeCodeForSession (callback/route.ts:56-57) does bounce to /login?error=reset_link_expired (line 60-61), rendering misleading reset-specific copy (line 27-28). It is a real inconsistency the other three handlers already guard against, but I refine severity to low: signInWithOAuth commits a full-page navigation that moots the second click in the common case, the verifier collision requires a sub-second double-click or a stalled redirect, and the worst outcome is a confusing-but-recoverable error message (user retries and succeeds), not data loss or a security hole.</sub>
- **[LOW]** OAuth callback exchange failure reports 'reset_link_expired' for non-reset flows — `src/app/auth/callback/route.ts:59-68`
  When exchangeCodeForSession fails for ANY reason, the route unconditionally redirects to /login?error=reset_link_expired (line 61). The same callback handles three distinct flows: Google OAuth signin/signup, email-confirmation signup (emailRedirectTo at api/auth/signup/route.ts:41), and password reset (next=/reset-password at LoginScreen.tsx:103). A failed OAuth exchange (e.g. the double-submit verifier mismatch in the finding above, or a user re-using a consumed OAuth code) surfaces to the user as 'This reset link has expired or was already used' (LoginScreen.tsx:27-29). That is a contradictory guardrail message: the user did not request a reset. It is cosmetic (no security impact — the deny is correct), but it will produce confused beta users and misleading support reports during the very flow most likely to break (OAuth).
  _→ Branch the error message on flow: if `next` was /reset-password use the reset-expired copy, otherwise use a generic 'Sign-in link expired or already used, please try again.' No behavioral change to the deny itself._
  <sub>verified (high): Confirmed present in current code. /auth/callback handles all three flows — Google OAuth (LoginScreen.tsx:57, no `next`), email-confirmation signup (api/auth/signup/route.ts:41 emailRedirectTo), and password reset (LoginScreen.tsx:103, next=/reset-password) — and all hit exchangeCodeForSession (route.ts:56-57). On any error, route.ts:59-68 unconditionally redirects to /login?error=reset_link_expired with no branch on flow type, which LoginScreen.tsx:27-28 renders as "This reset link has expired or was already used," so a failed OAuth or signup-confirm exchange shows a reset message the user never requested. The deny itself is correct, so it is a cosmetic/UX-and-support defect with no security impact — severity low is right. (The finding's file path for LoginScreen is wrong — it lives under src/components/onboarding/ and line numbers shifted — but the substance is accurate.)</sub>

#### Failure paths (3)

- **[MED]** OAuth allowlist gate fails closed by deleting a legitimately-allowlisted user during a transient Supabase blip — `src/app/auth/callback/route.ts:80-87 (via src/lib/beta-allowlist.ts:35-40)`
  In the OAuth signup path, a brand-new user (created within the 60s window) is checked against the allowlist at callback/route.ts:80. isEmailAllowlisted (beta-allowlist.ts:24-41) fails CLOSED: any Supabase query error returns false (line 35-40). When allowed===false, the route deletes the just-created auth.users row (line 87) and bounces to /waitlist?reason=not_allowlisted. Failure path: if the beta_allowlist SELECT hits a transient error (connection blip, brief Supabase outage, statement timeout) at the exact moment a genuinely-allowlisted user completes Google OAuth, that user is permanently deleted and told they are not on the beta. They have no session, no row, and no signal that this was a transient error rather than a real rejection. Fail-closed is the correct security posture for the gate decision, but coupling it to an irreversible deleteUser on a single transient read makes the blast radius a destroyed account rather than a retryable denial. The email signup path (signup/route.ts:30) shares the same fail-closed read but only returns a 403 — no deletion — so it is safe to retry; the OAuth path is the one that destroys state.
  _→ Distinguish 'confirmed not on allowlist' (data===null, error===null) from 'could not determine' (error!==null). On the could-not-determine case in the OAuth callback, do NOT delete the user — instead leave the account, sign them out, and redirect to a retryable error (e.g. /login?error=try_again) so a transient blip does not destroy a legitimate account. This needs isEmailAllowlisted to surface the error vs not-found distinction (today both collapse to false). High-stakes and irreversible — recommend a real human decision on the trade-off plus a dedicated test for the error-vs-absent branch._
  <sub>verified (high): Confirmed in code. isEmailAllowlisted (src/lib/beta-allowlist.ts:35-40) returns false on ANY query error (transient blip, statement timeout, brief outage), not just a genuine miss. In the OAuth path, callback/route.ts:80-81 reads that boolean and, on false for a user created inside the 60s NEW_USER_WINDOW_MS, calls admin.auth.admin.deleteUser(user.id) at line 87. The try/catch at 85-93 guards only the delete call itself, not the allowlist read, so a transient SELECT failure flows straight to an irreversible account deletion plus a /waitlist?reason=not_allowlisted bounce — the user is given a definitive 'not on the beta' signal for what was actually a retryable read failure. The contrast in the finding holds: the email path (signup/route.ts:30-33) shares the same fail-closed read but only returns a 403 with no deletion, so it is safely retryable. Severity stays medium, not blocker: fail-closed on the gate is the correct security posture and is documented as intentional (beta-allowlist.ts:18-23 says errors fail CLOSED, 'the beta gate is more important than uptime'); the defect is narrowly the coupling of that decision to an irreversible deleteUser on a single un-retried transient read. Likelihood is low (requires a Supabase error in the exact sub-second window between OAuth code exchange and the allowlist SELECT for a genuinely-allowlisted user) and beta blast radius is small, but the consequence — a destroyed account with no recovery signal — is real and currently present. This is a mechanical defect, not a design opinion. A minimal fix would distinguish 'not found' from 'query errored' (e.g. have isEmailAllowlisted throw on error, or return a tri-state) so the OAuth path can deny-without-delete (leave the orphan, let a returning attempt re-check) on a transient error rather than deleting; the orphan-cleanup rationale at lines 82-84 is better served by a sweep than by destroying state on an ambiguous read.</sub>
- **[LOW]** OAuth gate partial failure: deleteUser can fail while session cookies are cleared, orphaning the auth.users row — `src/app/auth/callback/route.ts:85-112`
  In the blocked-OAuth path, deleteUser is wrapped in try/catch (line 85-93) and the catch only logs — execution continues to the redirect (line 95) and cookie-clearing (line 104-111) regardless. Partial-failure scenario: deleteUser throws or fails (admin API hiccup, network), the catch swallows it, and the user is then signed out (cookies cleared) and bounced to /waitlist. Net result: an orphaned auth.users row exists for an email that is NOT on the allowlist and has no session. The code comment at line 82-84 states the deletion exists specifically 'so we don't accumulate orphaned users from blocked OAuth attempts' and 'so the email is free to join later via the waitlist' — both of those guarantees silently break on the swallowed-failure path. Consequence: if that same email is later added to beta_allowlist and tries to sign up via email/password, supabase.auth.signUp (signup/route.ts:38) will likely fail with 'user already registered' because the orphaned row still holds the email, and that raw Supabase message is surfaced verbatim to the user (signup/route.ts:47). Low severity because it requires an admin-API failure to trigger and the row is harmless until that email is allowlisted, but the swallowed error means there is no signal when it happens (log-only).
  _→ Either (a) treat a deleteUser failure as a hard error and surface it (so orphans are visible, not silent), or (b) document the orphan-row reconciliation expectation and add a periodic cleanup. At minimum, the log line at callback/route.ts:89 should be a level that is actually alertable so silent accumulation is detectable. Also add a test for the deleteUser-fails branch — auth/callback currently has zero test coverage (only signup/route.test.ts and waitlist/route.test.ts exist)._
  <sub>verified (high): Confirmed against the code. At src/app/auth/callback/route.ts:85-93 the deleteUser call is in a try/catch whose catch only console.errors; execution then falls through unconditionally to blockedRedirect (line 95) and cookie-clearing (lines 104-111), so a failed delete leaves an orphaned auth.users row while the session is cleared — exactly breaking the two guarantees stated in the code comment at lines 82-84. The downstream chain is logically sound: signup/route.ts gates on isEmailAllowlisted (line 30) before supabase.auth.signUp (line 38), so the orphan is dormant while the email is off the allowlist but, once that email is later allowlisted, signUp fires against the existing row and the raw 'user already registered' message is surfaced verbatim at line 47. No reconciliation/cleanup exists (only other deleteUser callers are the account-delete route and test teardown), and the only trace is a log. Low severity is correct: it requires an admin-API/network failure to trigger and is inert (no security/access impact — the allowlist fails closed) until the specific email is allowlisted and then attempts email/password signup.</sub>
- **[LOW]** Clients behind a proxy that strips forwarding headers share a single 'unknown' rate-limit bucket on the waitlist endpoint — `src/app/api/waitlist/route.ts:17-23,28`
  getClientIp falls back to the literal string 'unknown' when both x-forwarded-for and x-real-ip are absent (line 22). That value is passed straight to checkLimit as the rate-limit key (line 28). On Vercel x-forwarded-for is reliably populated, so this is benign in the normal deploy. But any path where the header is missing (a misconfigured proxy in front of Vercel, direct origin access, certain edge-case requests) collapses every such caller into one shared 3/hour bucket keyed on 'unknown'. Failure mode: a small number of header-stripped legitimate submissions can exhaust the shared bucket and 429 unrelated users who happen to share the same missing-header condition. This is a graceful-degradation gap, not a security hole — and it only bites if forwarding headers are ever absent in production, which on Vercel they are not. Flagging it because the lens is failure paths and 'unknown' is a silent shared-state collision.
  _→ Acceptable to leave as-is on Vercel given headers are guaranteed there. If you want defense in depth, either reject requests with no derivable IP (since legitimate Vercel traffic always has one) or widen the waitlist limit's tolerance. Low priority — confirm with whoever owns the Vercel/proxy config that x-forwarded-for is always present before treating this as fully closed._
  <sub>verified (high): The mechanical claim is accurate: getClientIp at src/app/api/waitlist/route.ts:22 returns the literal "unknown" when both x-forwarded-for and x-real-ip are absent, and that value is passed directly to checkLimit as the rate-limit key (line 28), collapsing all header-stripped callers into one shared 3/hour rl:waitlist bucket (rate-limit.ts:86) — so the silent shared-state collision genuinely exists in the code as written. However it is correctly rated low/Consider: deployment is Vercel (VERCEL_ENV gating in rate-limit.ts:35), where x-forwarded-for is platform-injected on every request, making the "unknown" branch effectively unreachable in production; the identical IP ladder at linq/webhook/route.ts:636-638 is used only for logging where collision is harmless; and the waitlist insert is idempotent (dedupe + 23505 catch), so even if hit it degrades to 429s for an unreachable caller class, not data loss or a security bypass. No documented discipline requires per-IP isolation here. Real and present, but a dormant graceful-degradation nit, not a ship blocker.</sub>

Assert: admin.auth.admin.deleteUser was called once with user.id; the response is a 307/redirect to a URL ending in /waitlist?reason=not_allowlisted; the Set-Cookie headers blank the session cookies (maxAge=0). Mock isEmailAllowlisted directly to isolate from the DB chain._

- **[MED]** OAuth callback: no test that a returning user (created_at older than 60s) skips the allowlist check entirely — `src/app/auth/callback/route.ts:75-77`

This is a data-integrity boundary the founder cannot verify manually.

#### Security (2)

- **[LOW]** Admin beta-allowlist route logs raw email to console — `src/app/api/admin/beta-allowlist/route.ts:104`
  On a successful insert the route runs console.log("[admin/beta-allowlist] inserted email=%s", email), writing a raw user email address to the server logs. The CLAUDE.md Security Rule names message content, phone numbers, and auth tokens; email is not in that explicit list, but the project's own structured-logging contract at src/lib/observability/log.ts:9 defines forbidden PII as 'message content, entry name, user email, phone numbers, any text the user typed.' So this line violates the codebase's stated PII-logging discipline. Every other admin route here logs only error.message (operational, not PII). This is the single PII-in-logs leak in the admin surface. Severity is low because the log is server-side (Vercel logs), admin-only context, and email is low-sensitivity relative to message content, but it is still a documented-discipline break. Confidence: high — the literal is right there and the log doc is explicit.
  _→ Drop the email from the log line. Log a success marker without PII, e.g. console.log("[admin/beta-allowlist] inserted (email_len=%d)", email.length) or just remove the line — the inserted row is already the source of truth. No test needed; this is a one-line redaction._
  <sub>verified (high): Confirmed against the code: src/app/api/admin/beta-allowlist/route.ts:104 contains `console.log("[admin/beta-allowlist] inserted email=%s", email)`, logging a raw normalized user email on successful insert. The project's structured-logging contract at src/lib/observability/log.ts:9 explicitly forbids logging "user email" and cites it as the CLAUDE.md PII rule, so this is a real break of a documented discipline. A grep across src/app/api confirms this is the only admin-surface log line that emits raw email — every other route logs only error.message or structured non-PII. Severity low is correct: server-side Vercel logs, admin-only path (requireAdmin gate), email low-sensitivity, and the email is one the admin themselves supplied.</sub>
- **[LOW]** confirm_failures stores raw user_id and admin route surfaces it (inconsistent with api_errors hashing) — `src/lib/observability/record-failure.ts:36 (write), src/app/api/admin/confirm-stats/route.ts:83 (read)`
  recordConfirmFailure inserts a raw user_id into public.confirm_failures, and /api/admin/confirm-stats returns it verbatim in the RecentFailure[] feed (interface field user_id: string at confirm-stats/route.ts:13-21). The sibling table api_errors deliberately hashes the id via hashUserId() before write (record-api-error.ts:32-33,41) so 'this table never stores raw ids.' The two observability tables disagree on whether a raw user_id is acceptable at rest. This is NOT a logging violation (the CLAUDE.md log rule governs console/log lines and message content, not DB columns, and a raw user_id is an ID, which the rule permits), and the table has RLS enabled (migration 20260417000004_confirm_failures.sql:27) plus the route is admin-gated, so there is no unauthorized exposure. Flagging it as a consistency/defense-in-depth gap rather than a breach: if confirm_failures were ever surfaced through a less-guarded path it would leak raw ids where api_errors would not. Confidence: high on the facts (raw id written and returned); medium on whether it matters — this is a judgment call the founder should make, since confirm_failures intentionally keeps message_id/conversation_id for cross-referencing a specific user's failure during debugging, which a hash would defeat.
  _→ Decide deliberately: either (a) accept raw user_id in confirm_failures and document why it differs from api_errors (debugging needs to join back to the user, RLS + admin gate is the control), or (b) hash it for parity. If kept raw, add a one-line comment in record-failure.ts noting the deliberate divergence so a future reviewer does not 'fix' it by copying the api_errors hashing and silently break the debugging join. No code change strictly required._
  <sub>verified (high): Every factual claim is confirmed in code: record-failure.ts:36 writes a raw user_id into confirm_failures, the admin_confirm_recent_failures RPC selects it raw (migration lines 86,101), and confirm-stats/route.ts:83 returns it in the RecentFailure[] JSON (typed user_id: string at lines 13-21) — while the sibling api_errors deliberately hashes via hashUserId() into user_id_hash (record-api-error.ts:32-33,41; migration line 18, comment 'so this table never stores raw ids'). The two observability tables genuinely disagree, and the finding's own caveats are all accurate: this is not the CLAUDE.md logging rule (which governs logs/message content, not DB columns, and permits IDs), RLS is enabled with no policies and the route is admin-gated via requireAdmin(), so there is no current unauthorized exposure, and confirm_failures keeps user_id as a real FK (migration line 14, index line 34) for legitimate per-user drill-down that a hash would defeat — a difference the schema-map doc at page.tsx:572 already frames as deliberate. One mild overstatement: the live UI (ConfirmHealthPanel.tsx:208-274) renders only error_kind/status_code/duration_ms/error_detail/created_at and never displays user_id, so 'surfaces it' is true at the API-payload layer but not on screen. Net: a real, accurately-described defense-in-depth/consistency gap, correctly self-rated as a low-severity judgment call for the founder rather than a breach.</sub>

#### Conflicts (2)

- **[LOW]** Out-of-order responses in openUserProfile / loadConversationMessages can render the wrong user's data — `src/lib/hooks/useAdminData.ts:158-192`
  openUserProfile sets selectedUser synchronously, then awaits POST /api/admin/conversations and /api/admin/manual, then unconditionally calls setUserConversations / setUserManual on resolve. There is no request-token or AbortController guard. If an admin clicks user A then user B before A's fetch resolves (slow service-role query on a large account), the slower response wins the last setState and lands the wrong user's conversation list and manual under the now-selected user. selectedUser shows B; the session/manual lists show A. The same pattern exists in loadConversationMessages (lines 203-221): rapid clicks across two sessions can render conversation A's messages + extraction state under selectedConversation B. Because every admin data view to user data also writes an admin_access_logs row (conversations:49, messages:43, manual:30), a misattributed view is a misattributed audit entry — the wrong target_user_id is what the access log shows, which matters for a product whose privacy posture is 'every conversation/message view is logged' (system.md:269). This is a UI/state-desync race, not data corruption; confidence high that the guard is absent, medium on real-world frequency (admin is single-operator, fast queries make the window narrow). Distinguish: mechanical defect in client state handling, not a product judgment.
  _→ Capture a request token (incrementing ref or AbortController) at the start of openUserProfile/loadConversationMessages keyed to the target id; on resolve, bail out of setState if the token/id no longer matches the current selection. Test: mock two sequential fetches resolving in reverse order and assert the final state matches the last-clicked user, and that no access-log misattribution occurs._
  <sub>verified (high): The mechanical race is genuinely present and unguarded: openUserProfile (useAdminData.ts:158-192) sets selectedUser synchronously then unconditionally calls setUserConversations/setUserManual on resolve, and loadConversationMessages (203-221) does the same for conversationMessages/extractionState — grep confirms zero AbortController/request-token/latest-request guards in the file, so a slower earlier click can transiently land the wrong user's lists under the now-selected user. The !profileLoading gate in UserProfilePane.tsx does not save it (the stale request's own finally sets profileLoading=false). However, the finding's severity hinges on its audit-misattribution claim, and that claim is false: admin_access_logs rows are written server-side keyed by the targetUserId/conversationId in each request body (conversations route ~48-52, messages route ~42-47), independent of which client setState wins — both requests fire and both log the correct target_user_id, so no audit entry is ever misattributed and the privacy posture at system.md:269 is not violated. The real blast radius is a transient, self-healing client-state desync where a single-operator, read-only admin may briefly see the wrong user's data on screen during rapid clicks; the final resolved state is always correct. That is a legitimate should-fix (add a per-call request token) but a low-severity UI race, not the medium privacy/audit issue the finding describes.</sub>
- **[LOW]** addToBeta optimistic waitlist removal is not rolled back when the cross-table delete fails — `src/lib/hooks/useAdminData.ts:248-260`
  On a successful allowlist insert the route returns result:'added' and, separately, attempts to delete the matching waitlist row. That waitlist delete is explicitly non-fatal server-side (beta-allowlist/route.ts:116-119 logs and continues) — the route still returns 'added' even if the waitlist row was NOT removed. The client then unconditionally filters that row out of local waitlist state (useAdminData.ts:252-258). Result: a client/server desync where the admin sees the email leave the waitlist, but the DB row still has status='waiting'. On next loadWaitlist (waitlistLoaded guards a reload, so it survives until a hard refresh) or via the /api/admin/waitlist/count badge (page.tsx:54), the row reappears, and the badge count contradicts the list the admin just saw. The waiting-count badge is loaded independently (page.tsx:51-63) and is never updated by addToBeta, so it is already inconsistent with the optimistic list mutation regardless of the delete outcome. Low severity: beta-list management, single operator, self-corrects on refresh; but it is a genuine state-desync between an optimistic mutation and the server's actual write.
  _→ Either have the POST return whether the waitlist delete succeeded and only filter local state on confirmed removal, or decrement/refetch waitingCount alongside the optimistic waitlist mutation so the badge and list cannot disagree. Test: mock POST returning 'added' with a waitlist delete error and assert the row is not silently dropped from local state without a corresponding count update._
  <sub>verified (high): Confirmed end-to-end. beta-allowlist/route.ts returns {result:"added"} at line 121 regardless of the waitlist delete, and the waitlist delete at 110-114 is explicitly non-fatal (deleteError logged and swallowed at 116-119). useAdminData.ts:248-258 then unconditionally filters the row out of local waitlist state with no reconciliation against the actual server write, and loadWaitlist (92-103) is guarded by waitlistLoaded with no force-reload, so on a failed delete the row reappears only on hard refresh; the page.tsx:51-63 count badge is fetched once and never updated by addToBeta, so it is stale even on the happy path and recounts the surviving row (status='waiting', the default per squash_baseline.sql:346) on the failed path. One wording inaccuracy in the claim — the route does a hard delete(), not a status='waiting' update — but the end-state it describes is correct because 'waiting' is the default and the row is never status-changed. Genuine optimistic-mutation desync; correctly scoped as low (single operator, beta admin, self-corrects on refresh, requires the uncommon insert-succeeds/delete-fails window).</sub>

#### Failure paths (4)

- **[LOW]** errors and confirm-stats routes have no try/catch — a thrown error escapes as an unhandled rejection / unstructured 500 — `src/app/api/admin/errors/route.ts:34-90`
  GET in errors/route.ts (and the identical confirm-stats/route.ts:31-88) is the only pair of admin routes with no surrounding try/catch. Two real throw sources sit inside the awaited calls: (1) requireAdmin() -> verifyAdmin() does `const { data: { user } } = await supabase.auth.getUser()` (src/lib/admin/verify-admin.ts:5-8) with no guard; if the Supabase Auth call rejects on a transport/network failure, the rejection propagates out of the route. (2) `admin.rpc(...)` is expected to resolve with `{data,error}` and the code handles `error` (lines 52-65), but a client-level throw (connection reset, malformed transport) is not caught. In either case the route throws instead of returning the documented `{error}` JSON + 500. Every sibling route (users, waitlist, beta-feedback, feedback, conversations, messages, manual, beta-allowlist, active-users) wraps its body in try/catch and returns `Response.json({error}, {status:500})`; these two diverge from that documented pattern (system.md 'Error Handling Pattern'). Downstream: the client callers (useAdminData.loadApiErrorsSummary at src/lib/hooks/useAdminData.ts:147-156 and the Confirm/Api panels) all check res.ok and fail silent, so the user sees a stale/zero badge rather than a crash — the blast radius is contained to a missing-data nav badge, which is why this is medium not high. But the inconsistency means an Auth-layer outage produces a different (unstructured) failure shape here than everywhere else, and it is the kind of gap that widens if these routes later gain logic.
  _→ Wrap both GET bodies in the same try/catch the other routes use, returning Response.json({error:'Internal server error'},{status:500}) on throw. Add a test (mirroring beta-feedback/route.test.ts 'returns 500 on auth list error') that makes requireAdmin/getUser reject and asserts a 500 JSON response rather than a thrown promise._
  <sub>verified (medium): The mechanical defect is present: errors/route.ts:34-90 and confirm-stats/route.ts:31-88 have no try/catch, and the throw sources are real (unguarded `await supabase.auth.getUser()` at verify-admin.ts:5-8, and admin.rpc client-level transport throws are uncaught), so on an Auth/transport outage these routes throw rather than returning {error}+500. But the finding's two load-bearing claims are FALSE: (1) it asserts these are "the only pair" without try/catch and "every sibling wraps its body" — actually FOUR admin routes lack try/catch (errors, confirm-stats, prompt-architecture/route.ts, waitlist/count/route.ts:9); the finding only checked 9 of the 15+ admin routes. (2) It cites system.md "Error Handling Pattern" as violated, but that section (system.md:199-205) only requires non-streaming routes to RETURN {error} JSON with a status code on failure — it mandates no try/catch and no unhandled-rejection discipline anywhere. So this is a uniform hardening gap across silent-failing badge endpoints (every consumer checks res.ok and swallows, e.g. useAdminData.ts:147-156, so blast radius is a stale/zero nav badge), not a unique two-route divergence from a documented rule. Severity refined down to low: the medium rating depended entirely on the "unique inconsistency violating the documented pattern" framing, which does not survive the repo.</sub>
- **[LOW]** admin_access_logs insert failure is silently swallowed — access happens but the audit row can be lost without signal — `src/app/api/admin/conversations/route.ts:49-53`
  In conversations/route.ts, messages/route.ts:43-48, and manual/route.ts:30-34 the access-log write is `await admin.from('admin_access_logs').insert({...})` with the result neither destructured nor error-checked. The Supabase client returns `{error}` on a DB failure (it does not throw for a normal query error), so a failed insert is silently dropped: the route still returns 200 with the user's conversations/messages/manual. system.md 'Admin Access' states 'Every conversation and message view is logged to admin_access_logs' and the flow map treats this as the audit trail. The failure path is: the admin successfully views a user's private conversation, the log insert fails (constraint, transient DB error), and there is no record that the view occurred and no console signal. This is an audit-completeness gap, not data loss of user content. Note the flow map's claim that this is 'fire-and-forget, no await' is inaccurate — it IS awaited; the issue is the unchecked result, not a dangling promise. (If the insert instead *threw* rather than returning {error}, the outer try/catch would convert a fully-successful data fetch into a 500 — the data was already read, so the admin would see a spurious error; worth noting but secondary.)
  _→ Destructure `const { error: logError } = await admin.from('admin_access_logs').insert(...)` and at minimum console.error on logError (IDs/counts only, never content, per the Security Rules). Decide deliberately whether a log-write failure should block the response: for an audit trail the safer posture is to log the failure loudly even if the data read still returns. A test asserting the route still 200s when the log insert returns an error (and that the failure is logged) would pin the chosen behavior._
  <sub>verified (high): Verified directly: all three routes write the access log as a bare `await admin.from('admin_access_logs').insert({...})` with the result neither destructured nor error-checked (conversations/route.ts:49-53, messages/route.ts:43-48, manual/route.ts:30-34). supabase-js returns {error} on normal DB failures rather than throwing — confirmed by every other insert in the codebase (waitlist:82, user/phone:141, webhooks/sendblue:283) destructuring and checking `error`, a pattern these three admin routes uniquely break. The reads complete before the insert, so on a failed log write the admin still receives the private data, the route returns 200, and no console signal is emitted — directly contradicting system.md:269 ('Every conversation and message view is logged to admin_access_logs') and the schema-map UI's treatment of this as a durable audit trail. The finding's self-corrections (it IS awaited; the throw-path is secondary because the data read precedes the insert) are also accurate. Real audit-completeness gap, not data loss or a security hole; low severity is appropriate. Fix is mechanical (destructure and console.error the result to match surrounding code).</sub>
- **[LOW]** beta-allowlist POST: waitlist-cleanup delete failure leaves a stale 'waiting' row but client optimistically removes it — `src/app/api/admin/beta-allowlist/route.ts:109-121`
  In the POST handler the allowlist insert and the waitlist-row delete are two separate, non-transactional writes. The delete error is explicitly treated as non-fatal (line 116-119, comment 'Non-fatal — the allowlist insert succeeded') and the route returns result:'added' regardless. On the client, addToBeta (src/lib/hooks/useAdminData.ts:248-260) sees result==='added' and unconditionally filters the row out of local waitlist state. Failure path: allowlist insert succeeds, waitlist delete fails (transient DB error) -> server still has a status='waiting' row for that email, but the admin's UI has removed it. The stale row resurfaces on next loadWaitlist and is also counted by /api/admin/waitlist/count (route.ts:14-17), so the Beta nav badge over-counts by one until manually re-resolved. This is a self-healing inconsistency (visible on refresh), hence low, and the non-fatal choice is defensible — but it is an unsurfaced partial-write the founder should know exists.
  _→ Either return a distinct signal when the waitlist delete failed (e.g. result:'added_waitlist_stale') so the client can avoid optimistically dropping the row, or accept the behavior and document it. No code change strictly required; the finding is that the partial-write is currently invisible to the operator._
  <sub>verified (high): Verified against source: in beta-allowlist POST (route.ts:95-121) the allowlist insert and waitlist delete are two non-transactional writes; the delete error is explicitly swallowed as 'Non-fatal' (line 116-119) and the route returns result:'added' regardless (line 121). Client addToBeta (useAdminData.ts:248-258) unconditionally filters the row out of local waitlist state on result==='added'. The waitlist/count route counts status='waiting' rows (count/route.ts:14-17), so a failed delete leaves a stale 'waiting' row that over-counts the badge by one until refresh. The partial-write is genuinely present and unsurfaced. Two nuances make it no worse than 'low': loadWaitlist early-returns on waitlistLoaded (useAdminData.ts:93) so the stale row reappears only on a full reload, not immediately (the list route returns all rows, so it does self-heal on refresh); and delete is the normal resolution path, so only a rare transient error triggers it. The non-fatal choice is defensible. Auth scoping itself is correct (requireAdmin on all handlers) — the defect is the partial write, not auth.</sub>
- **[LOW]** useIsAdmin initial false-state renders 'Not authorized' before getUser() resolves; getUser rejection leaves admin permanently locked out client-side — `src/lib/hooks/useIsAdmin.ts:4-18`
  useIsAdmin initializes isAdmin=false and only flips true after `supabase.auth.getUser()` resolves with role==='admin'. Two failure paths: (1) During the in-flight getUser() the admin page (src/app/admin/page.tsx:81-96) renders the 'Not authorized.' screen, then swaps to the dashboard — a brief incorrect-state flash on every load. (2) The `.then()` has no `.catch()`; if getUser() rejects (Auth transport failure), isAdmin stays false forever and the admin sees a permanent 'Not authorized.' with no retry and no distinction from a genuine permission denial. Neither is a security hole — the server gates independently via middleware (src/middleware.ts:40-48) and requireAdmin on every route, so a stale/failed client check cannot grant access. Impact is purely UX/observability for the operator, hence low. Worth noting the 'Not authorized.' copy conflates 'still loading', 'auth call failed', and 'genuinely not admin' into one message.
  _→ Track a tri-state (loading | admin | denied) instead of a boolean, render a neutral loading state while getUser() is in flight, and add a .catch that surfaces an auth-error state distinct from denial. Low priority; cosmetic given server-side enforcement._
  <sub>verified (high): Verified in source: useIsAdmin.ts:4-18 initializes isAdmin=false and only flips true inside an uncaught getUser().then() (lines 11-14, no .catch()), and admin/page.tsx:81-96 renders 'Not authorized.' whenever isAdmin is false. So both claims are currently present and correctly located: (1) a sub-second 'Not authorized.' flash on every admin load before getUser() resolves, and (2) on a getUser() rejection the operator is locked to 'Not authorized.' with no retry plus an unhandled rejection. The no-security-hole claim also holds — middleware.ts:40-48 gates /api/admin server-side and requireAdmin guards every admin route — so low severity is correct (operator-only UX/observability across the 14 useIsAdmin call sites). Caveat: sub-claim (1) is partly a design opinion since the getUser-over-getSession trade-off is intentional (comment at lines 9-10); sub-claim (2)'s silent permanent lockout is the stronger mechanical defect. Real, but a Consider, not a blocker.</sub>

#### Test gaps (5)

- **[MED]** No 403 / auth-gate test on the user-data routes that use the service-role (RLS-bypassing) client — `src/app/api/admin/conversations/route.ts:7-9; src/app/api/admin/messages/route.ts:7-9; src/app/api/admin/manual/route.ts:7-9; src/app/api/admin/beta-allowlist/route.ts:8,31,60`
  These routes call createAdminClient() (SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY, bypasses RLS per src/lib/supabase/admin.ts) and are gated only by requireAdmin(). The waitlist and beta-feedback tests prove the 403 pattern for THEIR handlers, but the highest-blast-radius routes — the ones that read arbitrary users' conversations, messages, and manual entries via the service role — have no test asserting a non-admin caller is rejected before any query runs. Per the flow map, middleware is the first gate and requireAdmin is the second; only the second is unit-testable here and it is untested for these handlers.
  _→ For each of POST /conversations, POST /messages, POST /manual, and GET/POST/DELETE /beta-allowlist: with verifyAdmin mocked to {userId:'',isAdmin:false}, assert res.status===403 AND that no admin query/insert (conversations select, messages select, manual_entries select, admin_access_logs insert, beta_allowlist insert/delete) was invoked. Reuse the requireAdmin mock shape from waitlist/route.test.ts:4-12._
  <sub>verified (high): Confirmed: all four routes destructure the service-role (RLS-bypassing) admin client from requireAdmin() — conversations/route.ts:7-9, messages/route.ts:7-9, manual/route.ts:7-9, beta-allowlist/route.ts:8,31,60 — and the client is built from SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY (admin.ts:13-14). No test file exists in any of these four directories, no test imports these handlers (grep empty), and verify-admin.ts itself has no unit test, so no test asserts a non-admin is rejected before a query runs; only waitlist/route.test.ts:62-92 and beta-feedback/route.test.ts:67-120 prove the 403 pattern, and only for their own handlers via a re-implemented requireAdmin mock. Refined high->medium: this is a real test/regression gap on the highest-blast-radius routes, but the guard code is present and correct in all four today, so nothing is currently broken — a should-fix, not a blocker. Caveat: middleware is the true first gate and is also untested at this layer; full assurance needs an integration test, not just a handler unit test.</sub>
- **[MED]** messages route 404-on-missing-conversation branch is untested — `src/app/api/admin/messages/route.ts:25-28`
  When the conversation lookup returns convError OR a null row, the route returns 404 'Conversation not found' and must short-circuit before the messages query and before the admin_access_logs insert. This is the only not-found path in the data-viewing routes and it gates whether an access-log row is written for a bogus conversationId.
  _→ With the conversations lookup mocked to {data:null,error:null}, POST {conversationId:'missing'} -> assert status 404 AND that no admin_access_logs insert occurred AND no messages select ran. Add a second case with {data:null,error:{message:'db'}} also yielding 404._

There is no route.test.ts for admin/messages and zero test-file references to 'admin/messages' (grep across *.test.ts) — the only importer is the client hook useAdminData.ts:207 — so the branch is genuinely untested. The access-log gating claim is accurate (messages, manual, conversations are the three routes inserting admin_access_logs, and the 404 sits upstream of the insert), and it is the only not-found path among data-viewing routes that touch user data. Real test gap, but it is a missing-coverage finding rather than a present defect (the code is correct today), the route is no less tested than most admin siblings, and the downstream impact of a regression is a spurious audit-log row behind requireAdmin, not a data/auth exposure — so medium is the correct severity, not higher.</sub>
- **[LOW]** No failure-path tests for the data-viewing routes (conversations/messages/manual) or the parallel-RPC routes (errors/confirm-stats/active-users) — `src/app/api/admin/conversations/route.ts:25-67`

  <sub>verified (high): Confirmed: all three routes write admin_id: userId from requireAdmin() (user.id via supabase.auth.getUser() at verify-admin.ts:13), never from the body; target_user_id in messages/route.ts:45 comes from a server-side conversation lookup. - **[LOW]** No test asserts requireAdmin sources userId from the server session, not the request — `src/lib/admin/verify-admin.ts:4-14, 24-32`
  verifyAdmin reads the user solely from createClient() (server SSR cookies) and derives isAdmin from user.app_metadata.role==='admin', returning {userId:'',isAdmin:false} when user is null. requireAdmin returns a 403 Response on failure or {userId, admin} on success. This is the shared auth primitive behind every admin route, yet there is no direct unit test for it (only indirectly, via mocks in waitlist/beta-feedback that REPLACE it). The null-user, wrong-role, and admin-role branches are never exercised against the real implementation, so a regression in the role check or a switch to reading role from a request-controlled source would pass CI.

Mock createAdminClient to a sentinel and assert it is only constructed on the success branch._
